Ground truth is the claim made by one of the two review articles; the evidence for every
component of that claim was verified to exist in a chunk of a cited collection-26 document
before the question was admitted (see build_questions.mjs).

The two arms hold the SAME 192 documents, the same 16,272 chunks and the same embeddings.
Retrieval was confirmed identical, chunk for chunk, so the knowledge graph is the only
variable between them.

RUBRIC
  CORRECT — conveys the expected claim. Equivalent wording counts; extra correct detail
            does not penalise.
  PARTIAL — right direction but incomplete: part of a multi-part answer missing, or the
            fact surfaced without being committed to.
  WRONG   — contradicts the expected claim, or fails to answer it.

Q015  cross_doc
  QUESTION : Which representation do WyckoffDiff and WyCryst both build on, and what advantage does
             that representation give a generative model?
  EXPECTED : Both build on the Wyckoff-position representation of crystal symmetry, which lets the
             model generate symmetric structures by construction rather than having to learn
             symmetry from coordinates.
  c26 CORRECT : Both WyckoffDiff and WyCryst build on a Wyckoff-based representation [8]. Regarding
               the advantages of this representation for a generative model, the corpus provides the
               following information: * **WyCryst:** The Wyckoff representation is described as an
               "excellent choice" for invertible generative models [8]. Furthermore, the ability to
               identify equivalent Wyckoff positions without explicit training serves as evidence
               that the representation, when used with a PVAE, possesses an understanding of crystal
               symmetry [8]. The framework is designed to learn from sampling based on rules of
               symmetry rather than random sampling [8]. * **WyckoffDiff:** The corpus does not
               explicitly state the specific advantages of the Wyckoff-based representation for
               WyckoffDiff's generative capabilities, other than noting that the model starts from
               all Wyckoff positions to generate the elements that occupy them [8].
  c33 WRONG   : The provided corpus does not state that WyckoffDiff and WyCryst build on the same
               representation. In fact, it notes that WyCryst is based on a "different
               representation" than WyckoffDiff [1!]. Regarding the advantages of the Wyckoff
               representation used by WyCryst, the corpus states that it is an "excellent choice for
               both forward learning models and invertible generative models" [7]. Additionally, the
               corpus indicates that the critical information about a material is embedded in the
               symmetries of the crystal structure, which are sufficient to describe a material's
               stability, band structure, and synthesizability [7]. Furthermore, the ability to
               identify equivalent Wyckoff positions without explicit training serves as evidence
               that the Wyckoff representation, when used with a PVAE, possesses an understanding of
               crystal symmetry, allowing for sampling from latent space based on rules of symmetry
               rather than random sampling [7]. The corpus does not explicitly describe the
               advantages of the representation used by WyckoffDiff in the same manner.
         why : denied that WyckoffDiff and WyCryst share the Wyckoff representation, calling WyCryst
               a 'different representation'

Q017  cross_doc   [G] cited
  QUESTION : CGCNN and MEGNet are both graph networks for crystals. What does MEGNet add beyond atom
             and bond updates that CGCNN does not have?
  EXPECTED : MEGNet introduces a global state attribute updated alongside atom (node) and bond
             (edge) features, so information is consolidated into a whole-material state vector;
             CGCNN updates only atom and bond representations.
  c26 PARTIAL : The provided corpus does not explicitly detail the specific architectural differences
               between MEGNet and CGCNN regarding what MEGNet adds beyond atom and bond updates.
               While the corpus notes that MEGNet is a generalization of CGCNN [5][G], it describes
               the MEGNet architecture as containing a global state vector ($u$) in addition to
               atomic ($V$) and bond ($E$) attributes [5]. It also explains that in the MEGNet
               module, information flows among atoms, bonds, and the global state vector, with the
               global state attributes being updated in the third step of the module [3]. However,
               the corpus does not provide a comparative analysis of the CGCNN architecture to
               confirm whether or not CGCNN lacks these specific global state features or update
               mechanisms.
         why : identified MEGNet's global state vector but refused to confirm CGCNN lacks it
  c33 CORRECT : MEGNet models incorporate a global state vector (u) in addition to atomic (V) and
               bond (E) attributes [3]. This global state vector is used to store molecule- or
               crystal-level attributes, such as the temperature of the system [7]. Within a MEGNet
               module, the global state attributes are updated through information flow alongside
               the bond and atomic attributes [3]. Furthermore, MEGNet allows for the incorporation
               of state variables—such as temperature, pressure, and entropy—as global state inputs
               [10]. The provided documents do not explicitly detail the internal architecture of
               CGCNN to confirm which of these specific features it lacks. The corpus states only
               that graph networks are a "generalization/superset" of models like CGCNN [10].

Q019  single_doc
  QUESTION : ElemNet predicts formation enthalpy from composition alone. How many OQMD compounds was
             it trained on, and what mean absolute error did it achieve?
  EXPECTED : It was trained on about 275,000 OQMD compounds and achieved a mean absolute error of
             roughly 0.050 eV/atom.
  c26 PARTIAL : ElemNet was trained on 256,622 compounds [6!]. The model achieved a mean absolute
               error (MAE) of 0.050 ± 0.000 eV/atom on its test set [1]. Another section of the
               document reports an MAE of 0.055 eV/atom for a test set [1].
         why : MAE 0.050 right; gave 256,622 training compounds, the paper's figure, against the
               review's 275,000
  c33 WRONG   : The provided documents do not state the specific number of OQMD compounds used to
               train ElemNet. While the text mentions that the models were tested on a set of 25,662
               compounds [2, 6!] and that it scanned a composition space of 450 million [3, 5!], it
               does not explicitly provide the total count of compounds in the training set.
               Regarding the mean absolute error (MAE) achieved by ElemNet: * In one test set,
               ElemNet achieved an MAE of 0.055 eV/atom [1]. * In another analysis of a test set
               containing 25,662 compounds, the model achieved an MAE of 0.050 ± 0.000 eV/atom [2].
         why : gave neither the training-set size nor the MAE, only the 25,662-compound test set

             database?

             about 60,000 Materials Project crystals.
  c26 CORRECT : The MP-20 dataset is a subset of the Materials Project [G]. Regarding the number of
               crystals MEGNet used for training from the Materials Project: * For formation energy
               models, MEGNet was trained on 60,000 crystals [2]. * For elastic moduli models,
               MEGNet was trained on 12,179 crystals [2]. * More broadly, the formation energy data
               set available in the Materials Project comprises approximately 69,000 crystals [1].
  c33 WRONG   : The provided corpus does not mention a dataset named "MP-20." Therefore, it does not
               address which database such a dataset is drawn from. Regarding the Materials Project
               database, which is mentioned in the corpus, MEGNet models were trained on varying
               amounts of data depending on the specific property: * For formation energy, MEGNet
               models were trained on 60,000 crystals [2]. Another section notes that the formation
               energy dataset comprises approximately 69,000 crystals [1]. * For elastic moduli,
               MEGNet models were trained on 12,179 crystals [2].
         why : replied that the corpus does not mention MP-20 at all; the graph arm knew it is a
               Materials Project subset

Q046  cross_doc
  QUESTION : Why does a benchmark of generative crystal models need a novelty measure in addition to
             a stability measure?
  EXPECTED : Because a model can score well on stability by reproducing structures already in the
             training database; the stable, unique and novel (S.U.N.) rate is used so that only
             genuinely new stable structures count.
  c26 CORRECT : Benchmarks of generative crystal models require both stability and novelty measures
               because they serve distinct purposes in evaluating the model's performance and
               utility: * **Stability measures** (such as RMSD, formation energy, or energy above
               the convex hull) are used to assess the physical viability of the generated
               structures [1, 5, 9!]. For example, RMSD measures local stability by comparing
               generated structures to DFT-relaxed counterparts [1!], while energy above the convex
               hull is used to determine if a material is thermodynamically stable [9!]. * **Novelty
               measures** are used to assess the model's ability to generate new materials that are
               not present in the training dataset [2, 5, 9!]. * **The need for both:** While
               stability ensures that the generated materials are physically plausible, novelty is
               necessary to confirm that the model is actually discovering new materials rather than
               simply memorizing or reproducing the training data [2, 4, 5!]. In practical materials
               discovery, the goal is to identify novel materials [2]. The corpus does not
               explicitly state a single, unified reason why these two metrics must be used
               *together* beyond the implicit understanding that a model could theoretically
               generate stable but unoriginal structures (memorization) or novel but unstable
               structures (physically invalid). However, it does note that in a practical materials
               discovery setting, the interest lies in novel materials [2!], and that models can
               sometimes "memorize" the training distribution, which increases
               stability/faithfulness but decreases novelty [4!].
  c33 PARTIAL : The provided corpus does not explicitly state why a benchmark *needs* both a novelty
               measure and a stability measure in combination. However, it defines the distinct
               purposes of these metrics: * **Novelty:** This metric is used to quantify a model's
               proficiency in generating materials that do not exist in the training dataset [5, 6,
               9!]. In practical materials discovery, the primary interest is in finding these novel
               materials [2!]. Additionally, measuring novelty helps determine if a model is simply
               "memorizing" the training distribution [4!]. * **Stability:** This metric is used to
               evaluate the physical viability or structural integrity of the generated materials
               [1, 5, 9!]. For example, RMSD measures local stability by comparing generated
               structures to DFT-relaxed counterparts [1!], while formation energy and energy above
               the convex hull are used to assess global stability [5, 9!]. Because certain metrics,
               such as FWD, can benefit from sampling materials from the training set, researchers
               also compute metrics on only novel materials to assess whether the model is faithful
               to the training distribution while still producing new structures [2, 3!].
         why : defined novelty and stability separately but declined to say why both are needed

Q081  cross_doc   [G] cited
  QUESTION : What claim about deep learning does the NequIP result challenge, and what does its data
             efficiency make newly practical?
  EXPECTED : It challenges the widely held belief that deep neural networks require massive training
             sets, and its data efficiency makes it practical to build potentials from high-order
             quantum chemical reference data and run high-fidelity molecular dynamics over long
             timescales.
  c26 CORRECT : The NequIP results challenge the notion that deep learning models require massive
               datasets to achieve high performance. While traditional deep learning approaches
               often rely on large training sets, NequIP demonstrates "exceptional data efficiency"
               by achieving performance comparable to or better than other methods using
               significantly fewer data points [1, 2, 3!]. For instance, NequIP models trained on as
               few as 133 structures can perform effectively on extended systems like liquid water
               and ice, whereas previous models required 133,500 reference calculations—a 1,000-fold
               reduction in data requirements [8!]. This high data efficiency makes it newly
               practical to: * **Apply deep learning to extended systems with fewer resources:** By
               requiring 1,000 times less data than previous models (such as DeepMD), NequIP makes
               it feasible to train accurate interatomic potentials for complex systems like liquid
               water and ice using much smaller datasets [8]. * **Achieve high accuracy with minimal
               training data:** NequIP can produce results competitive with or superior to models
               trained on 50 times more data, as seen in comparisons with SchNet on molecules like
               aspirin and naphthalene [1]. * **Enable efficient inference:** By balancing accuracy
               and computational efficiency, NequIP potentials can be trained to provide efficient
               inference, which is useful for materials discovery [4]. Additionally, the use of
               equivariant convolutions and tensor features is identified as the mechanism enabling
               this high data efficiency [3]. NequIP is also noted to outperform other methods,
               including DeepMD and SchNet [1, 8!][G].
  c33 PARTIAL : Based on the provided documents, the corpus does not explicitly state a specific
               "claim about deep learning" that NequIP challenges in a general sense. However, the
               documents provide evidence regarding NequIP's data efficiency compared to other
               methods: * **Data Efficiency:** NequIP demonstrates "significantly improved data
               efficiency in comparison with existing methods" [2, 3!]. For example, NequIP models
               trained on as few as 50 to 250 data points achieved root mean square errors (RMSEs)
               comparable to or better than other methods trained on significantly larger datasets
               [2, 3!]. * **Practical Applications:** The data efficiency of NequIP makes it
               practical to train models on significantly smaller datasets than previously required.
               For instance, in a study involving liquid water and ice structures, a NequIP model
               was trained using only 133 structures, which is 1,000 times fewer data points than a
               previously trained DeepMD neural network interatomic potential (NNIP) model that
               required 133,500 reference calculations [8]. The corpus does not address what
               specific broader claim about deep learning is being challenged by these results.
         why : said the corpus states no general claim that NequIP challenges; the graph arm
               answered it

Q094  multi_hop   [G] cited
  QUESTION : One paper trains machine-learning potentials for crystal structure prediction
             deliberately using structures that are not ordered. Why would disordered structures
             help a potential meant for ordered crystals?
  EXPECTED : Training on disordered structures covers a far wider region of configuration space than
             relaxed ordered crystals, so the potential remains accurate on the unrelaxed,
             far-from-equilibrium configurations that structure search actually visits.
  c26 CORRECT : The provided corpus indicates that constructing training sets for machine-learning
               potentials (MLPs) using disordered structures—specifically from
               density-functional-theory (DFT) based dynamical trajectories of liquid and quenched
               amorphous phases—is a strategy to overcome the "dearth of information on the crystal
               structure" that typically complicates the selection of training data [5]. Using this
               approach offers the following advantages: * **No prior structural knowledge
               required:** This method does not require any preceding information about the
               material's structure, only its chemical composition [5]. * **Efficiency:** This
               approach can significantly reduce the computational time required to construct the
               training set [9]. * **Effective ranking and prediction:** Despite being trained on
               disordered phases, the resulting neural network potentials (NNPs) show strong
               correlations with DFT energies for both experimental phases and theoretically
               generated low-energy crystal structures [10]. This ensures the NNPs can properly rank
               energies among low-energy crystalline structures and identify metastable phases more
               efficiently than DFT-based approaches [10]. Machine learning potentials are used for
               Crystal Structure Prediction [G].
  c33 WRONG   : The provided documents do not explicitly explain *why* disordered structures are used
               to train machine-learning potentials (NNPs) for the purpose of predicting ordered
               crystals, other than noting that the proposed method constructs training sets from
               "density-functional-theory (DFT) based dynamical trajectories of liquid and quenched
               amorphous phases" [10]. Regarding the methodology and its benefits, the corpus
               provides the following information: * **Lack of prior information:** The approach of
               using liquid and quenched amorphous phases does not require any preceding information
               about the material's crystal structure, only its chemical composition [10]. *
               **Efficiency:** Using neural network potentials (NNPs) is advantageous for increasing
               the Z number (the number of formula units in a unit cell) because they utilize
               order-N scaling, which is more favorable than the order-N³ scaling associated with
               DFT [3]. * **Performance:** The resulting NNPs can properly rank energies among
               low-energy crystalline structures and allow for the identification of metastable
               phases more efficiently than DFT-based approaches [10]. The corpus does not address
               the specific theoretical mechanism or reasoning behind why disordered structures
               specifically assist in the prediction of ordered crystals.
         why : declined to explain why disordered structures help a potential meant for ordered
               crystals (0/2 support docs)

Q107  multi_hop
  QUESTION : Two different papers here classify crystal symmetry from X-ray diffraction. One works
             from simulated patterns, the other from a limited number of experimental thin-film
             patterns. How does the second overcome its data scarcity?
  EXPECTED : By coupling supervised learning with a physics-informed, model-agnostic data
             augmentation strategy that uses simulated ICSD data alongside the experimental
             patterns.
  c26 CORRECT : The second paper, which focuses on classifying crystal symmetry from a limited number
               of experimental thin-film X-ray diffraction (XRD) patterns, overcomes the problem of
               data scarcity by using a supervised machine learning approach coupled with a
               model-agnostic, physics-informed data augmentation strategy [4]. This strategy
               involves the following components: * **Combining Data Sources:** The framework
               utilizes both experimental and simulated XRD patterns to train the machine learning
               classification algorithm [1]. The simulated dataset is generated by extracting
               crystal structure information from the Inorganic Crystal Structure Database (ICSD)
               [1]. * **Data Augmentation:** The datasets undergo data augmentation based on three
               specific spectral transformations [1, 10!]. This helps address the differences
               between experimental thin-film patterns and simulated, idealized, randomly-oriented
               patterns [2!]. * **Supervised Learning:** The experimental dataset consists of
               synthesized samples that are manually labeled for training and testing purposes [1,
               10!]. The corpus does not provide details on the specific "three spectral
               transformations" beyond noting their existence [1, 10!].
  c33 PARTIAL : The provided corpus describes a single framework for rapid XRD classification that
               addresses the problem of data scarcity when working with novel thin-film materials
               [4]. It overcomes this limitation by combining two strategies: * **Coupling
               supervised machine learning with data augmentation:** The framework uses a supervised
               machine learning approach paired with a "model-agnostic, physics-informed data
               augmentation strategy" [4]. * **Integrating simulated and experimental data:** The
               training process utilizes both experimental XRD patterns (which are manually labeled)
               and simulated XRD patterns extracted from the Inorganic Crystal Structure Database
               (ICSD) [4]. The datasets are then subjected to data augmentation based on three
               specific spectral transformations [4].
         why : treated the XRD work as a single framework rather than contrasting the two papers'
               data sources

Q127  cross_doc
  QUESTION : Both a molecular VAE and an inorganic-compound VAE in this corpus report a validation
             difficulty. What is the shared difficulty, and how does each handle it?
  EXPECTED : Judging whether generated candidates are realistic: the molecular VAE relies on
             chemical validity of decoded structures and a property predictor, while the Binded-VAE
             had to define novel metrics because image metrics like FID and Inception scores do not
             apply.
  c26 CORRECT : The corpus identifies a shared difficulty regarding the validity of the generated
               outputs, though the specific nature of this validity differs between the two
               contexts. **The Shared Difficulty** Both models face the challenge of ensuring that
               the points generated in the latent space or the final output correspond to valid,
               realistic structures [1, 6!]. * **Molecular VAE:** For molecular VAEs, the difficulty
               is ensuring that all points in the latent space correspond to valid molecules [6!]. *
               **Inorganic-compound VAE:** For inorganic material generation, the difficulty is that
               standard generative models often fail to produce compounds that are considered
               "realistic" or valid according to expert rules [1, 5!]. **How Each Handles It** *
               **Molecular VAE:** The corpus does not describe the specific mechanism used to handle
               this validity issue for molecular VAEs, other than noting that it is a desirable
               property for any application of VAEs in chemistry [6!]. * **Inorganic-compound VAE:**
               This model handles the difficulty through several strategies: * **Architecture:** It
               uses a "Binded-VAE" (BVAE) model, which splits the learning process into two chained
               VAEs: a "Mask-VAE" that generates a binary mask (indicating component presence) and a
               "Ratio-VAE" (a Conditional VAE) that generates the specific ratios of those
               components [2, 3, 8!]. * **Evaluation:** It employs a novel "Expert Rules Agreement"
               (ERA) metric, which measures the percentage of generated data that adheres to
               specific rules defined by experts (such as bounds on component proportions) [3, 10!].
               This metric is used because traditional validation processes like FID and Inception
               scores are tailored for images and are insufficient for this context [5!].
  c33 WRONG   : The corpus does not identify a shared validation difficulty between the molecular VAE
               and the inorganic-compound VAE. Regarding the inorganic-compound VAE: *
               **Difficulty:** The primary difficulty is generating compounds that are realistic,
               given that the data is characterized by discrete features and severe sparsity [3].
               Standard VAEs fail to generate compounds with a few non-zero components and adequate
               ratios because of this sparsity [3]. Furthermore, traditional validation processes
               like FID and Inception scores are tailored for images and cannot be used directly for
               this context [3]. * **Handling:** The authors introduce a "Binded-VAE" (BVAE) model
               that learns to jointly generate the raw data mask (a binary vector indicating
               component presence) and the component ratios [3]. They also propose three new metrics
               tailored to compound generation: an adapted FID (FID*), the KL divergence between
               real and generated property distributions, and an "Expert Rules Agreement" (ERA)
               metric to measure the percentage of generated data that matches expert-defined rules
               [6]. Regarding the molecular VAE: * The provided excerpts mention that for any
               application of VAEs in chemistry, it is desirable that all points in the latent space
               correspond to valid molecules [6]. However, the corpus does not explicitly describe a
               "validation difficulty" for the molecular VAE, nor does it describe how the molecular
               VAE handles such a difficulty.
         why : said the corpus identifies no shared validation difficulty between the two VAEs

Q129  enumerate   [G] cited
  QUESTION : Which molecular and crystal string representations are discussed in this corpus
             alongside SMILES, and what is each one's defining property?
  EXPECTED : SMILES, a compact text encoding whose strict syntax can produce invalid strings;
             SELFIES, a 100% robust encoding where every string is a valid molecule; and SLICES, a
             line-input crystal encoding used by MatterGPT for inorganic solids.
  c26 CORRECT : The corpus discusses the following molecular string representations alongside SMILES:
               * **SELFIES (Self-Referencing Embedded Strings):** Its defining property is that it
               is a 100% robust molecular string representation, meaning every possible string
               corresponds to a valid molecule [9]. It achieves this by using derivation rules that
               enforce chemical valence-bond validity and prevent syntactically invalid strings [7].
               The corpus does not discuss any specific crystal string representations. While the
               provided relationships mention that "CGCNN maps crystal" and "crystal contains Atom,"
               these relationships do not identify a specific string representation for crystals
               [G].
  c33 PARTIAL : The corpus discusses the following molecular string representation alongside SMILES:
               * **SELFIES (Self-Referencing Embedded Strings):** Its defining property is that it
               is a "100% robust" representation, meaning every SELFIES string corresponds to a
               valid molecule, even if the string is entirely random [10]. It uses derivation rules
               to enforce the validity of chemical valence-bonds [7!] and stores information
               regarding branch length and ring size alongside specific identifiers [1!]. The corpus
               does not address crystal string representations.
         why : gave SELFIES only, omitting SLICES

Q138  cross_doc
  QUESTION : Which representation choice do a Wyckoff-based transformer and a Wyckoff-based
             diffusion model share, and what does it let both of them guarantee?
  EXPECTED : Both represent a crystal by its space group and the occupied Wyckoff positions rather
             than by explicit atomic coordinates, which guarantees the generated structure has a
             valid crystallographic symmetry by construction.
  c26 PARTIAL : Based on the provided documents, both the Wyckoff-based transformer and the
               Wyckoff-based diffusion model (specifically the WyckoffGNN architecture) utilize a
               representation that treats Wyckoff positions as nodes in a graph [10]. This
               representation choice allows both models to utilize a single model architecture
               across all space groups, despite the fact that different space groups contain varying
               numbers of Wyckoff positions [10]. By using a graph representation and processing it
               with a graph neural network (GNN), the model gains the flexibility to handle these
               variations [10].
         why : Wyckoff positions as graph nodes; missed that the representation guarantees valid
               symmetry by construction
  c33 WRONG   : Based on the provided documents, the corpus does not identify a specific
               representation choice shared by both a Wyckoff-based transformer and a Wyckoff-based
               diffusion model, nor does it state what such a shared choice would guarantee for
               both. While the documents discuss Wyckoff-based representations for both types of
               models, they do so in separate contexts: * **Wyckoff-based Transformer:** The
               transformer model uses a representation that decomposes structures into composition,
               stability, structure, lattice, and Wyckoff positions [3]. It uses a shared
               representation for common orbits, resulting in 990 unique Wyckoff positions across
               all space groups [5]. * **Wyckoff-based Diffusion Model:** The diffusion model
               (specifically "WyckoffDiff") uses a graph neural network (WyckoffGNN) where each
               Wyckoff position is viewed as a node in a fully connected graph [10]. This approach
               allows the model to utilize a single model for all space groups because it provides
               the flexibility to handle the varying number of Wyckoff positions across different
               space groups [10]. The provided text does not explicitly compare these two approaches
               to identify a shared choice or a shared guarantee.
         why : said the corpus identifies no shared representation between the Wyckoff transformer
               and diffusion model

Q139  multi_hop
  QUESTION : Two papers in this corpus generate crystals with a transformer conditioned on the space
             group. What is generated after the space group is fixed, and how do the two differ in
             output?
  EXPECTED : After the space group is chosen, both place species into Wyckoff positions; the
             space-group informed transformer emits full crystal structures for arbitrary space
             groups, while the Wyckoff material transformer emits a Wyckoff-level protostructure
             that is then relaxed.
  c26 WRONG   : The provided corpus does not contain two distinct papers that generate crystals using
               a transformer conditioned on the space group. The excerpts describe a single model,
               "CrystalFormer," which is a space group-informed transformer [2]. While the corpus
               mentions other models in the context of comparing them to CrystalFormer, it does not
               provide information about a second paper that generates crystals using a transformer
               conditioned on the space group. The corpus discusses the following models in relation
               to CrystalFormer: * **DiffCSP++:** This model exploits space group symmetry but does
               not predict Wyckoff positions during generation; instead, it requires searching for
               template structures in the training set [3]. * **Ref. [!]:** This approach conditions
               the generation of CIF on space group symbols in a language model setting but does not
               provide exact constraints on the space group [3]. * **Ref. [!]:** This approach uses
               a fine-tuned generative model with space group labels but does not provide exact
               constraints on the space group [3]. * **Ref. [!]:** This approach generates symmetric
               crystals in their Wyckoff representation but does not consider fractional coordinates
               and lattice parameters, requiring a subsequent computational search [3]. Because the
               corpus does not describe a second paper that uses a transformer conditioned on the
               space group, it cannot compare the outputs of two such papers. Regarding the
               CrystalFormer model described in the corpus, once the space group number ($g$) is
               fixed, the model generates the following: * **Wyckoff letters:** These are sampled
               sequentially [5]. * **Chemical species:** These are sampled sequentially [5]. *
               **Fractional coordinates:** These are sampled sequentially [5]. * **Lattice

               data are mentioned: * **Unsupervised Pre-training:** The SMILES Transformer addresses
               the issue of poor performance in small datasets by using unsupervised pre-training of
               a sequence-to-sequence language model on a large corpus of SMILES (a text
               representation system for molecules) [8!]. This pre-training facilitates good
               generalization in small-data settings [8!]. * **Multi-task Pre-training:** The DPA-2
               model utilizes multi-task pre-training on a large corpus of datasets to improve
               generalizability to downstream tasks [10!]. This allows for "zero-shot
               generalization," where the model is tested directly on downstream tasks without
               needing further refinement data [10!]. * **Domain-specific Pre-training:** Models can
               be pre-trained on large, domain-specific text corpora before being applied to
               downstream tasks [5]. For example, MatSciBERT is pre-trained on a large corpus of
               materials science text [5], and MatBERT is pre-trained on a corpus of materials

               generalization in small-data settings [8]. For example, the SMILES Transformer learns

  QUESTION : Which crystal generative models are evaluated on the MP-20 benchmark across this
             corpus, and what metric is used to compare their crystal-structure-prediction
             performance?
  EXPECTED : CDVAE, DiffCSP, FlowMM and CrystalFlow (among others) are all evaluated on MP-20; they
             are compared by match rate and RMSE computed with StructureMatcher.
  c26 : Based on the provided corpus, the following crystal generative models are evaluated on the
         MP-20 benchmark: * **CrystalFlow:** Evaluated on MP-20 [4]. * **CDVAE (Crystal Diffusion
         Variational Autoencoder):** Evaluated on MP-20 [5][G]. * **DiffCSP:** Evaluated on MP-20
         [5, 7!][G]. * **FlowMM:** Evaluated on MP-20 [7, 10!]. * **FTCP:** Evaluated on MP-20 [G].
         * **G-SchNet:** Evaluated on MP-20 [G]. * **MatterGen-MP:** Trained on MP-20 [G]. *
         **DiffCSP-C and DiffCSP-D:** Evaluated on MP-20 [G]. The predictive performance for crystal
         structure prediction (CSP) on the MP-20 dataset is evaluated using the **match rate (MR)**
         and the **root mean squared error (RMSE)** [1]. The match rate is defined as the fraction
         of structures in the test set that are successfully predicted [7]. To calculate these
         metrics, *k* candidate structures are generated for each test set structure, and the model
         is evaluated based on whether any of the generated structures match the ground truth [1].
         The Pymatgen library's `StructureMatcher` function is used to evaluate similarity between
         structures [7]. The corpus does not address whether other generative models mentioned in
         the text (such as P-cG-SchNet) are evaluated on MP-20, though it notes that P-cG-SchNet is
         compared against diffusion-based models like CDVAE and DiffCSP regarding computational cost
         [5!].
  c33 : Based on the provided corpus, the following crystal generative models are evaluated on the
         MP-20 benchmark: * **CrystalFlow:** Evaluated on MP-20 [4]. * **CDVAE:** Evaluated on MP-20
         [5, 7!]. * **DiffCSP:** Evaluated on MP-20 [5, 7!]. * **FlowMM:** Evaluated on MP-20 [7,
         10!]. The predictive performance for crystal structure prediction (CSP) on the MP-20
         dataset is evaluated using the following metrics: * **Match Rate (MR):** This is defined as
         the fraction of structures in the test set that are successfully predicted [7]. For each
         structure in the test set, *k* candidate structures are generated, and the model determines
         if any match the ground truth [1]. * **Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE):** This is calculated
         between the positions of matched atom pairs, normalized by the volume derived from the
         average lattice parameters [7]. The corpus does not provide information regarding other
         generative models evaluated on MP-20 beyond those listed above.

Q016 cross_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts   support 1/2
  QUESTION : SELFIES was introduced to fix a specific failure of SMILES. What is that failure, and
             what does SELFIES guarantee instead?
  EXPECTED : SMILES strings can be syntactically invalid, so a generative model can emit strings
             that do not correspond to any molecule. SELFIES is a 100% robust representation in
             which every string maps to a valid molecule.
  c26 : SELFIES was introduced to address the issue that a significant fraction of SMILES strings
         do not correspond to valid molecules [3]. These SMILES strings are often either
         syntactically invalid (they do not correspond to a molecular graph) or they violate basic
         chemical rules, such as the maximum number of valence bonds between atoms [3]. In contrast,
         SELFIES guarantees that every generated string corresponds to a valid molecule [9].
         Consequently, SELFIES is 100% robust, meaning that 100% of the generated molecule
         candidates are valid [9].
  c33 : SELFIES was introduced to address the problem that a significant portion of SMILES strings
         do not correspond to valid molecules [3]. These invalid SMILES strings are either
         syntactically incorrect—meaning they do not correspond to a molecular graph—or they violate
         basic chemical rules, such as the maximum number of valence bonds between atoms [3]. In
         contrast, SELFIES guarantees that every generated string corresponds to a valid molecule
         [9]. While SMILES strings have a low probability of validity when subjected to random
         mutations (e.g., 9.9%, 3.0%, and 1.1% for one, two, and three mutations, respectively),
         SELFIES are valid in 100% of cases [4].

  QUESTION : NequIP claims a large data-efficiency advantage. How much less training data does it
             need than existing models, and what architectural choice produces that advantage?
  EXPECTED : Up to three orders of magnitude fewer training data, achieved by using E(3)-equivariant
             convolutions over geometric tensors instead of invariant convolutions acting only on
             scalars.
  c26 : NequIP demonstrates significant data efficiency compared to existing models, in some cases
         requiring up to three orders of magnitude less training data [8]. For example, in
         experiments involving water and ice, NequIP was trained on 133 structures, which is 1,000
         times fewer data points than the 133,500 reference calculations used to train a DeepMD
         model [4]. The high data efficiency of NequIP is attributed to the following architectural
         choices: * **Equivariant Convolutions:** The use of E(3)-equivariant convolutions for
         interactions of geometric tensors provides a more faithful and information-rich
         representation of atomic environments compared to models that use invariant convolutions
         acting only on scalars [8]. Specifically, the use of tensor (vector) features and
         equivariant convolutions is what enables this high data efficiency [3]. * **Multiple Labels
         per Configuration:** Each training configuration contains multiple labels (atomic forces),
         which increases the total number of labels available beyond just the potential energy label
         associated with each structure [6]. The corpus does not specify the exact amount of
         training data required for all existing models compared to NequIP, though it notes that
         NequIP can outperform SchNet trained on 50 times more molecules in some instances (e.g.,
         aspirin and naphthalene) [1]. Additionally, the corpus indicates that NequIP outperforms
         DeepMD [G] [4!].
  c33 : NequIP demonstrates a significant data-efficiency advantage, with the corpus noting that it
         can achieve state-of-the-art accuracy using up to three orders of magnitude less training
         data than existing models [8]. For example, in experiments involving liquid water and ice,
         NequIP was trained using 133 structures, which is 1,000 times fewer data points than the
         133,500 reference calculations used for a previously trained DeepMD model [4]. This high
         data efficiency is attributed to the use of E(3)-equivariant convolutions [3].
         Specifically, the model employs tensor (vector) features and equivariant convolutions to
         interact with geometric tensors, which provides a more information-rich and faithful
         representation of atomic environments compared to contemporary models that rely on
         invariant convolutions and scalar-only actions [8]. The corpus notes that the equivariant
         setting (using $l=0$ and $l=1$ interactions) significantly outperforms the invariant
         setting (using only $l=0$) across all data set sizes [3].

Q031 single_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts, [G] cited   support 1/1
  QUESTION : What is the central architectural innovation of SchNet, and why was it needed for
             molecules?
  EXPECTED : Continuous-filter convolutional layers, needed because atoms in molecules are not
             restricted to a grid and discretizing their precise positions would lose essential
             physical information.
  c26 : The central architectural innovation of SchNet is the use of continuous-filter convolutions
         [1]. In this architecture, interactions between atoms are modeled by separate atom-wise
         layers [7], where the molecule is represented atom-wise, analogous to pixels in an image
         [7]. The filter-generating network uses interatomic distances to model the molecules [9].
         This architecture was needed for molecules to address several requirements: * **Modeling
         Quantum Interactions:** SchNet is designed to learn representations for predicting
         molecular energies and atomic forces [1]. * **Physical Invariance and Equivariance:** The
         architecture reflects fundamental physical laws, including invariance to atom indexing and
         translation [1]. Furthermore, its energy predictions are rotationally invariant, and its
         force predictions are equivariant [1]. * **Smoothness and Conservation:** The model ensures
         smooth energy prediction with respect to atom positions and maintains energy conservation
         in the predicted force fields [1]. The corpus does not explicitly state why
         "continuous-filter" convolutions specifically were chosen over other methods, other than
         noting that SchNet is a continuous-filter convolutional neural network [3][G].
  c33 : The central architectural innovation of SchNet is the use of continuous-filter convolutions
         [1]. This architecture treats molecules by representing them atom-wise, which is analogous
         to how pixels are treated in an image [7, 9!]. Regarding why this approach is needed for
         molecules, the corpus provides the following information: * **Modeling Quantum
         Interactions:** SchNet is designed to learn representations specifically for predicting
         molecular energies and atomic forces [1]. * **Physical Properties:** The architecture is
         designed to reflect fundamental physical laws, including invariance to atom indexing and
         translation [1]. Furthermore, its energy predictions are rotationally invariant, and its
         force predictions are equivariant [1]. * **Handling Interactions:** The network uses three
         interaction blocks to model interatomic interactions [9]. These interactions between
         feature maps are handled by separate atom-wise layers [3]. * **Distance-Based Modeling:**
         The model achieves rotational invariance and equivariance by utilizing interatomic
         distances within the filter network to model molecules [9].

Q035 cross_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts, [G] cited   support 1/2
  QUESTION : Why is a mobile robot argued to be preferable to a fixed automated platform for
             materials research?
  EXPECTED : Because materials research demands a diversity of sample types, operations, instruments
             and measurements that fixed platforms cannot cover, so a mobile robot that moves
             between existing instruments generalizes where a bespoke rig does not.
  c26 : Based on the provided documents, a mobile robot is argued to be preferable to fixed
         automated platforms for materials research for the following reasons: * **Flexibility and
         Modularity:** The mobile robot is designed as a flexible platform that can be used for a
         wide range of research tasks beyond the specific experiments demonstrated [1]. Its modular
         approach allows for the straightforward addition of further instruments, such as NMR or
         X-ray diffraction, without requiring physical modifications to the laboratory [2]. *
         **Compatibility with Existing Laboratories:** Unlike fixed systems that might require
         inconvenient modifications to a laboratory (such as lowering bench heights), the mobile
         robot has human-like dimensions and reach, allowing it to operate in conventional,
         unmodified laboratory environments [3]. * **Efficiency and Speed:** The autonomous workflow
         is significantly faster than manual methods (1000 times faster) and at least 10 times
         faster than semiautomated, non-autonomous robotic workflows [6]. It can operate for up to
         21.6 hours per day with optimal scheduling [3]. * **Capability for Complex Tasks:** The
         robot can dispense both insoluble solids and liquid solutions with high accuracy and
         repeatability, whereas many automated systems are limited to dispensing only liquids [3].
         It is also capable of dexterous manipulations comparable to those performed by human
         researchers, such as handling sample vials and operating instruments [3]. * **Autonomous
         Search Capabilities:** The robot can autonomously explore large multivariate spaces, which
         is advantageous for problems where current understanding is insufficient to reduce the
         number of candidate components [9]. It uses a batched Bayesian search algorithm to search

  QUESTION : Which diffusion-based crystal generative models in this corpus explicitly incorporate
             crystallographic symmetry, and what is each one's mechanism for doing so?
  EXPECTED : SymmCD, via the asymmetric unit plus learned symmetry transformations; WyckoffDiff, via
             a Wyckoff-position representation in a discrete diffusion model; and the space-group
             informed transformer, via space-group conditioning and Wyckoff placement. DiffCSP by
             contrast diffuses lattice and fractional coordinates without explicit space-group
             constraints.
  c26 : Based on the provided corpus, two diffusion-based generative models explicitly incorporate
         crystallographic symmetry: **1. SymmCD** * **Mechanism:** SymmCD decomposes a crystal into
         two components and learns their joint distribution through diffusion: the asymmetric unit
         (the smallest subset of the crystal capable of generating the whole via symmetry
         transformations) and the symmetry transformations required for each atom in that unit [1].
         It uses a novel, interpretable representation of these transformations as binary matrices,
         which allows for information-sharing and generalization across different crystal and site
         symmetries [4]. The model performs joint diffusion over the lattice representation,
         fractional coordinates of atoms, atom types, and the binary representation of site symmetry
         [10]. **2. WyckoffDiff** * **Mechanism:** WyckoffDiff generates symmetry-based descriptions
         of crystals by utilizing a crystal structure representation that encodes all symmetry [7].
         It employs a novel neural network architecture designed to use this representation within a
         discrete generative model framework [7]. The model generates protostructures using a
         representation that enables the application of generative models for discrete data to
         create new materials [2]. It explicitly encodes symmetry to allow the model to generate
         within a space of materials with higher symmetry [2]. The corpus does not address whether
         other diffusion-based models mentioned in the relationships, such as Crystal Diffusion
         Variational Autoencoder (CDVAE), explicitly incorporate symmetry in the same manner; in
         fact, one excerpt notes that CDVAE does not incorporate knowledge about symmetry [6!].
      why: named SymmCD and WyckoffDiff but not the space-group informed transformer, and drew no
           contrast with DiffCSP
  c33 : Based on the provided corpus, two diffusion-based generative models explicitly incorporate
         crystallographic symmetry: **1. SymmCD** * **Mechanism:** SymmCD decomposes a crystal into
         two components: the asymmetric unit (the smallest subset of the crystal capable of
         generating the whole through symmetry transformations) and the symmetry transformations
         required for each atom within that unit [1]. It learns the joint distribution of these
         components through diffusion [1]. To enable generalization across different

Q037 cross_doc   c26 PARTIAL   |   c33 PARTIAL   graph 25 facts, [G] cited   support 2/2
  QUESTION : Two distinct papers in this corpus both present a flow-matching generative model for
             crystals. What distinguishes the base distribution used by each?
  EXPECTED : CrystalFlow flows from simple analytic prior distributions over lattice, fractional
             coordinates and atom types; FlowLLM instead uses a fine-tuned large language model as
             the base distribution, then refines its samples with Riemannian flow matching.
  c26 : The two papers in the corpus presenting flow-matching generative models for crystals are
         *FlowMM* (2406.04713.pdf) and *FlowLLM* (2410.23405.pdf). Their base distributions differ
         significantly in their construction and source: * **FlowMM:** This model uses a base
         distribution defined as a product of distributions across atomic types ($A$), fractional
         coordinates ($F$), and lattice parameters ($L$) [8]. Specifically: * The base distribution
         for atomic types ($a$) follows the approach of Chen et al. (2022), using a normal
         distribution $N(a; 0, 1)$ [8!][G]. * The base distribution for fractional coordinates ($f$)
         is a uniform distribution, $Uniform(0, 1)$, which covers the torus [8]. * The model
         leverages the flexibility of Flow Matching to choose an informative base distribution for
         the lattice parameters ($l$) [8]. * **FlowLLM:** This model utilizes a Large Language Model
         (LLM) as its base distribution ($p_0:= p_{LLM}$) [2]. * Because the LLM base distribution
         is not constrained to the manifold $C$, it can occasionally generate invalid crystals,
         though this is rare (approximately 0.5% rejection rate) [7]. * The LLM base distribution is
         discrete rather than continuous, as it relies on finite precision representations (e.g.,
         fractional coordinates represented with only two decimal places) [7]. * The use of a
         learned base distribution allows FlowLLM to converge in as few as 50 integration steps [6].
      why: answered for FlowMM and FlowLLM rather than CrystalFlow and FlowLLM — a defensible
           reading of the question
  c33 : The two papers in the corpus that present flow-matching generative models for crystals are
         *FlowMM* (2406.04713.pdf) and *FlowLLM* (2410.23405.pdf) [4]. The base distributions used
         by each model are distinguished as follows: * **FlowMM:** This model uses a base
         distribution on the Riemannian manifold $C$ that is defined as a product of distributions
         on atomic types ($A$), fractional coordinates ($F$), and lattice parameters ($L$) [8].
         Specifically: * The base distribution for atomic types $p(a)$ is a normal distribution,
         $N(a; 0, 1)$ [8]. * The base distribution for fractional coordinates $p(f)$ is a uniform
         distribution, $\text{Uniform}(0, 1)$, which covers the torus [8]. * The model leverages
         flexibility in choosing an informative base distribution for the lattice parameters $l$
         [8]. * **FlowLLM:** This model uses a Large Language Model (LLM) as its base distribution,
         denoted as $p_0:= p_{\text{LLM}}$ [2]. * Because the LLM base distribution is not
         constrained to the manifold $C$, it can occasionally generate invalid crystals, which the
         authors handle by rejecting those samples [7]. * The LLM base distribution is discrete,
         taking non-zero values only over a small number of points due to finite precision
         representations (e.g., fractional coordinates represented with only two decimal places)
         [7]. * The use of a learned base distribution allows FlowLLM to converge in as few as 50
         integration steps [6].
      why: answered for FlowMM and FlowLLM rather than CrystalFlow and FlowLLM

Q040 single_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts   support 1/1
  QUESTION : In the space-group informed transformer, how is a crystal constructed once a space
             group has been chosen?
  EXPECTED : Atoms are selected from the periodic table and placed sequentially into the Wyckoff
             positions of the chosen space group within the unit cell.
  c26 : Once a space group has been chosen, the CrystalFormer model constructs a crystal through
         the following process: * **Control and Embedding:** The vector embedding of the chosen
         space group number ($g$) acts as a control variable that directs all subsequent outputs of
         the transformer, including Wyckoff letters, chemical species, fractional coordinates, and
         lattice parameters [7]. * **Sampling Procedure:** In each step of the sampling process, the
         model makes choices regarding Wyckoff positions, atom species, and fractional coordinates
         (if they remain unspecified) [10]. * **Role of Space Group and Wyckoff Letters:** The space
         group determines the specific meaning of Wyckoff letters, which define multiplicities and
         fractional coordinates. These are used to place the correct number of atoms precisely
         within the unit cell [7]. * **Constraint Handling:** The crystal lattice is expressed as
         constraints on the space group and occupied Wyckoff letters [2]. * **Coordinate
         Periodicity:** The fractional coordinates are treated as periodic variables following a von
         Mises distribution to respect the periodicity of the crystal [4].
  c33 : Once a space group number ($g$) is chosen, the construction of a crystal in the
         CrystalFormer model proceeds as follows: * **Control Variable:** The vector embedding of
         the space group number $g$ acts as a control variable that directs all subsequent outputs
         of the transformer, including Wyckoff letters, chemical species, fractional coordinates,
         and lattice parameters [7]. * **Sampling Procedure:** During each step of the sampling
         process, the model makes choices regarding the Wyckoff positions, atom species, and any
         remaining unspecified fractional coordinates [10]. * **Role of Space Group and Wyckoff
         Letters:** The space group determines the specific meaning of Wyckoff letters, specifically
         regarding multiplicities and fractional coordinates. This information is used to place the
         correct number of atoms precisely within the unit cell [7]. * **Handling Coordinates:** The
         periodicity of fractional coordinates is respected by treating them as periodic variables
         that follow a von Mises distribution [4]. * **Lattice Parameters:** The lattice parameters
         of the conventional unit cell are also generated as part of the process [10]. The corpus
         does not provide further details on the specific sequence or algorithmic steps beyond these
         components.

Q044 cross_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts, [G] cited   support 2/2
  QUESTION : What does StructureMatcher do in the evaluation of crystal generative models, and which
             tolerance settings are used for the match-rate calculation?
  EXPECTED : StructureMatcher decides whether a generated structure matches the ground-truth
             structure within tolerances; FlowMM reports settings stol = 0.5, angle_tol = 10, ltol =
             0.3.
  c26 : `StructureMatcher` is a function within the `pymatgen` library used to compare crystal
         structures and measure their similarity [1, 4, 9!][G]. It is employed in the evaluation of
         crystal generative models to assess how well generated structures align with ground-truth
         or target structures [2, 5, 7, 9!]. Specifically, `StructureMatcher` performs the following
         functions: * **Match Rate Calculation:** It determines the fraction of generated structures
         that are considered "matching" or "similar" to a reference set (such as ground-truth
         structures or held-out test sets) based on defined tolerance thresholds [2, 4, 5, 7, 9!]. *
         **RMSE/RMSD Calculation:** When structures are identified as a match, it computes the
         Root-Mean-Square Error (RMSE) or Root-Mean-Square Distance (RMSD) between the positions of
         matched atom pairs, often normalized by the average interatomic spacing (derived from cell
         volume and atom count) [2, 4, 5, 7, 9!][G]. * **Novelty and Uniqueness Assessment:** It is
         used to determine if a generated structure is novel by comparing it against training
         datasets, and to enforce uniqueness by grouping similar generated structures into
         equivalence classes [3, 10!][G]. **Tolerance Settings** For the calculation of the match
         rate, studies frequently use the following standard tolerance settings: * `ltol` (lattice
         tolerance): 0.3 [2, 5, 7, 9!] * `stol` (site tolerance): 0.5 [2, 5, 7, 9!] * `angle_tol`
         (angle tolerance): 10 degrees [2, 5, 7, 9!] While these are common, some applications use
         the "default settings" of `StructureMatcher` without explicitly specifying these values [3,
         4, 10!].
  c33 : In the evaluation of crystal generative models, `StructureMatcher` is used to assess the
         similarity between structures [1, 7, 9!]. Specifically, it performs the following
         functions: * **Matching Structures:** It determines if two structures are similar based on
         defined tolerance parameters [1, 7, 9!]. This is used to calculate the "Match Rate" (the
         fraction of generated structures that successfully match a target, such as a ground-truth
         structure) [2, 5, 7, 9!]. * **Calculating Error:** When structures are determined to be a
         match, `StructureMatcher` is used to compute the Root-Mean-Square Error (RMSE) or RMSD
         between the generated structure and the ground-truth or ground-state structure [2, 4, 5, 7,
         9!]. * **Assessing Novelty and Uniqueness:** It is used to identify novel structures by
         comparing generated structures against training datasets [3, 10!]. It also performs
         pairwise comparisons between generated structures to group similar ones into equivalence
         classes, ensuring that only unique structures are counted when calculating metrics like the
         S.U.N. (Stable, Unique, and Novel) rate [3, 10!]. **Tolerance Settings** For match-rate
         calculations, studies consistently use the following threshold parameters for
         `StructureMatcher`: * **ltol (lattice tolerance):** 0.3 [2, 5, 7, 9!] * **stol (site
         tolerance):** 0.5 [2, 5, 7, 9!] * **angle_tol (angle tolerance):** 10 [2, 5, 7, 9!] Some
         applications, such as assessing novelty or comparing generated structures to ground states,
         may use the "default settings" of `StructureMatcher` rather than the specific thresholds
         listed above [3, 4, 10!].

Q046 cross_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 PARTIAL   graph 25 facts   support 0/2
  QUESTION : Why does a benchmark of generative crystal models need a novelty measure in addition to
             a stability measure?
  EXPECTED : Because a model can score well on stability by reproducing structures already in the
             training database; the stable, unique and novel (S.U.N.) rate is used so that only
             genuinely new stable structures count.
  c26 : Benchmarks of generative crystal models require both stability and novelty measures because
         they serve distinct purposes in evaluating the model's performance and utility: *
         **Stability measures** (such as RMSD, formation energy, or energy above the convex hull)
         are used to assess the physical viability of the generated structures [1, 5, 9!]. For
         example, RMSD measures local stability by comparing generated structures to DFT-relaxed
         counterparts [1!], while energy above the convex hull is used to determine if a material is
         thermodynamically stable [9!]. * **Novelty measures** are used to assess the model's
         ability to generate new materials that are not present in the training dataset [2, 5, 9!].
         * **The need for both:** While stability ensures that the generated materials are
         physically plausible, novelty is necessary to confirm that the model is actually
         discovering new materials rather than simply memorizing or reproducing the training data
         [2, 4, 5!]. In practical materials discovery, the goal is to identify novel materials [2].
         The corpus does not explicitly state a single, unified reason why these two metrics must be
         used *together* beyond the implicit understanding that a model could theoretically generate
         stable but unoriginal structures (memorization) or novel but unstable structures
         (physically invalid). However, it does note that in a practical materials discovery
         setting, the interest lies in novel materials [2!], and that models can sometimes
         "memorize" the training distribution, which increases stability/faithfulness but decreases
         novelty [4!].
  c33 : The provided corpus does not explicitly state why a benchmark *needs* both a novelty
         measure and a stability measure in combination. However, it defines the distinct purposes
         of these metrics: * **Novelty:** This metric is used to quantify a model's proficiency in
         generating materials that do not exist in the training dataset [5, 6, 9!]. In practical
         materials discovery, the primary interest is in finding these novel materials [2!].
         Additionally, measuring novelty helps determine if a model is simply "memorizing" the
         training distribution [4!]. * **Stability:** This metric is used to evaluate the physical
         viability or structural integrity of the generated materials [1, 5, 9!]. For example, RMSD
         measures local stability by comparing generated structures to DFT-relaxed counterparts
         [1!], while formation energy and energy above the convex hull are used to assess global
         stability [5, 9!]. Because certain metrics, such as FWD, can benefit from sampling
         materials from the training set, researchers also compute metrics on only novel materials
         to assess whether the model is faithful to the training distribution while still producing
         new structures [2, 3!].
      why: defined novelty and stability separately but declined to say why both are needed

  QUESTION : What hard constraints can a GFlowNet-based crystal sampler enforce that an
             unconstrained diffusion model cannot, and why does that matter for generated
             candidates?
  EXPECTED : It can enforce physicochemical and geometric hard constraints during sampling — for
             example charge neutrality and space-group validity — so generated crystals are valid by
             construction rather than filtered afterwards.
  c26 : A GFlowNet-based crystal sampler, such as Crystal-GFN, can enforce several hard constraints
         that existing generative models often struggle to incorporate [3]. These constraints are
         implemented by restricting the action space of the GFlowNet, both within and across the
         subspaces of space group, composition, and lattice parameters [4]. Specific hard
         constraints include: * **Charge Neutrality:** The model ensures that all generated
         compositions have a neutral charge [1]. * **Composition-Space Group Compatibility:** The
         model restricts the sampling of compositions to those compatible with the Wyckoff positions
         of the selected space group [1]. For example, certain restrictive space groups cannot
         accommodate compositions with fewer than 16 atoms per element [1]. * **Lattice Parameter
         Validity:** The model enforces constraints between the space group and the lattice
         parameters to ensure the generated unit cell shape is valid [8]. **Why this matters for
         generated candidates:** Existing generative models for crystal structures often fail to
         embed essential physicochemical constraints, which frequently results in the generation of
         physically unrealistic and unstable structures [3]. By incorporating domain knowledge from
         materials science and crystallography to impose these hard compatibility and validity
         constraints, Crystal-GFN ensures that the sampled crystal structures are always valid,
         leading to more physically viable structures [3]. When these constraints are not applied,
         validity rates can be extremely low; for instance, one experiment showed a 100% success
         rate for lattice parameter validity with constraints, compared to only 0.41% for a GFlowNet
         trained without them [9!].
  c33 : Based on the provided corpus, the following hard constraints can be enforced by a
         GFlowNet-based crystal sampler (specifically Crystal-GFN): * **Neutral Charge:** The
         sampler incorporates a hard constraint to ensure all generated compositions have a neutral
         charge [1]. * **Composition-Space Group Compatibility:** The sampler restricts the action
         space to only allow compositions compatible with the Wyckoff positions of the selected
         space group [1]. This is necessary because symmetry constraints mean not all compositions
         are valid for every space group (e.g., space group 230 cannot accommodate compositions with
         fewer than 16 atoms per element) [1]. * **Lattice Parameter Validity:** The sampler
         enforces inter-subspace constraints between the space group and the lattice parameters (the
         crystal system defines the relations between the six lattice parameters) [8]. **Why this
         matters for generated candidates:** Existing generative models for crystal structures often
         struggle to embed these essential physicochemical constraints, which frequently results in
         physically unrealistic and unstable structures [3]. By incorporating domain knowledge from
         materials science and crystallography to impose these hard compatibility and validity
         constraints, Crystal-GFN ensures that the sampled crystal structures are always valid,
         leading to more physically viable structures [3, 9!]. For example, when these constraints
         are applied, the sampler achieves a 100% success rate in structural checks, compared to a
         0.41% success rate for a GFlowNet trained without such constraints [9!]. The provided
         corpus does not contain information regarding the specific capabilities or limitations of
         "unconstrained diffusion models" in this context, and therefore cannot provide a comparison
         between them and GFlowNet-based samplers.

Q053 single_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts, [G] cited   support 1/1
  QUESTION : What problem with standard cross-domain GANs motivated CrystalGAN, and what does
             CrystalGAN generate?
  EXPECTED : Materials science needs data of higher order complexity than the observed samples,
             which state-of-the-art cross-domain GANs cannot produce directly; CrystalGAN generates
             new chemically stable crystallographic structures with increased domain complexity.
  c26 : Standard cross-domain GANs, such as DiscoGAN and CycleGAN, are limited because they cannot
         generate data with increased complexity [2]. In the field of materials science, there is a
         specific need to synthesize data with higher-order complexity than what is observed in
         existing samples, and state-of-the-art cross-domain GANs cannot be adapted directly to meet
         this requirement [3]. CrystalGAN generates the following: * **Novel stable chemical
         structures:** It is designed to generate new, chemically stable crystallographic structures
         [3]. * **Ternary compounds:** Specifically, it generates ternary stable chemical structures
         [1] and ternary compositions [G]. * **Pseudo-binary samples:** As part of its process, it
         generates pseudo-binary samples [1] [G]. * **Higher-order complexity data:** It produces
         data with increased domain complexity compared to observed binary compounds [3].
  c33 : The motivation for developing CrystalGAN and its output are as follows: **Problem with
         standard cross-domain GANs** * Standard cross-domain GANs, such as DiscoGAN and CycleGAN,
         are unable to generate data with increased complexity [2]. * In the field of materials
         science, there is a specific need to synthesize data with higher-order complexity compared
         to observed samples, and existing state-of-the-art cross-domain GANs cannot be adapted
         directly to meet this need [3]. **What CrystalGAN generates** * CrystalGAN generates novel,
         chemically stable ternary crystallographic structures [1]. * It produces data with
         increased domain complexity compared to observed samples [3]. * Specifically, it generates
         ternary structures where the domains are well-separated from observed binary compounds [9].
