         MCMC. While the corpus notes that GFlowNets are an alternative to MCMC [5] and are related
         to amortized variational inference [5], it does not identify a training objective using
         that specific analogy. Regarding the second part of your question, GFlowNets amortize the
         expensive stochastic "search" typically performed by MCMC methods to reach low-energy
         configurations [2]. In MCMC, this involves generating a long sequence of samples to
         approach a desired distribution [7], or performing a lengthy stochastic search in the space
         of objects [7]. GFlowNets replace this with an upfront training phase, allowing them to
         generate samples in a single, short trajectory during inference [2]. This process is
         described as amortizing the work done by MCMC [G].
      why: amortising MCMC right; never named the detailed balance objective
  c33 : The provided corpus does not explicitly name a specific training objective that is
         introduced by analogy with MCMC. While the documents discuss GFlowNets as an alternative to
         MCMC and mention that GFlowNets use "amortized inference" [2, 5, 8!], they do not identify
         a specific training objective by that name. Regarding the expensive computation that
         GFlowNets amortize, the corpus states that GFlowNets replace the "expensive stochastic
         'search' (to reach low energy configurations) normally performed by MCMC" with a training
         phase [2]. This allows GFlowNets to avoid the "lengthy stochastic search in the space of
         such objects and the associated mode-mixing intractability challenge of MCMC methods" [4].
         Instead of MCMC's approach of generating a long sequence of samples to approach a desired
         distribution, GFlowNets perform amortized training so that, during inference, a sample can
         be generated in a single short trajectory [4].

Q115 multi_hop   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts   support 2/2
  QUESTION : GFlowNets sample in proportion to a reward. What does that proportionality buy compared
             with reward maximization, and how is it exploited in crystal sampling?
  EXPECTED : It yields a diverse set of high-reward candidates rather than collapsing onto a single
             optimum; Crystal-GFN exploits this to sample diverse valid crystals with low formation
             energy or a target band gap.
  c26 : Sampling in proportion to a reward, rather than maximizing it, provides several advantages
         for GFlowNets: * **Diversity of Samples:** Unlike methods that focus on a single mode (such
         as standard reinforcement learning or variational inference), GFlowNets are designed to
         sample from a distribution proportional to the reward [3]. This allows them to discover
         multiple modes of a reward function, which is a key objective in scientific discovery [3,
         9!]. * **Robustness and Uncertainty:** Because GFlowNets generate a diverse set of samples,
         they can be used to incorporate epistemic uncertainty and provide robustness against proxy
         misspecification in settings where a cheap proxy is used to evaluate samples before a more
         expensive true reward function is applied [5!]. * **Avoiding Mode-Mixing Intractability:**
         GFlowNets avoid the "mode-mixing intractability" challenge associated with MCMC methods,
         which often struggle to move between different modes of a distribution [6, 9!]. By
         amortizing the training of a generative policy, GFlowNets can generate multiple independent
         and identically distributed (i.i.d.) samples in one shot, rather than requiring a long
         sequence of samples that gradually approach the target distribution [4, 6, 10!]. Regarding
         the exploitation of this proportionality in crystal sampling, the provided corpus does not
         contain specific details on the mechanics of how crystal sampling exploits this
         proportionality beyond noting that GFlowNets (specifically "Crystal-GFN") are used for
         sampling crystals with desirable properties and constraints [9!]. The corpus does not
         detail the specific algorithms or techniques used within the crystal sampling domain to
         leverage the proportional sampling property.
  c33 : Sampling in proportion to a reward, rather than simply maximizing it, provides several
         advantages: * **Diversity of Samples:** GFlowNets are designed to discover multiple modes
         of a reward function [3]. While methods like reinforcement learning or variational
         inference tend to focus on a single mode, GFlowNets excel in scenarios where the reward
         function is highly multimodal, allowing for a diversity of samples [3]. * **Robustness and
         Uncertainty:** In contexts where a cheap proxy for the true reward function is used, the
         diversity of samples generated by GFlowNets allows for robustness against proxy
         misspecification and the incorporation of epistemic uncertainty [5]. * **Avoiding
         Mode-Mixing Issues:** Unlike MCMC methods, which can suffer from mode-mixing intractability
         (the difficulty of moving between different modes of a distribution), GFlowNets avoid
         lengthy stochastic searches in the space of objects [6]. * **Amortized Inference:**
         GFlowNets exchange the intractability of MCMC sampling for the challenge of amortized
         training. Once trained, they can generate samples in a single pass through a sequence of
         stochastic steps, rather than requiring a long sequence of samples to approach the desired
         distribution [9]. Regarding the exploitation of this proportionality in crystal sampling,
         the provided corpus does not contain specific details on how crystal sampling exploits this
         proportionality beyond noting that GFlowNets have been successfully applied to molecular
         property prediction and biological sequence design as part of active learning algorithms
         [9]. While one excerpt mentions "Crystal-GFN: sampling crystals with desirable properties
         and constraints" in its title [9], the text does not elaborate on the specific mechanics of
         how crystal sampling utilizes the proportionality to reward.

  QUESTION : A transformer was pretrained on SMILES to produce molecular fingerprints. What problem
             with rule-based fingerprints motivated it, and in which data regime does it help most?
  EXPECTED : Rule-based fingerprints map molecules to a sparse discrete space and perform poorly for
             shallow models or small datasets; the pretrained SMILES transformer helps most in
             small-data settings.
  c26 : The SMILES Transformer was motivated by the fact that conventional, rule-based molecular
         fingerprints—which map molecules to a sparse discrete space—perform poorly when used with
         shallow prediction models or small datasets [3]. The SMILES Transformer is most effective
         in small-data settings, where its unsupervised pre-training facilitates good generalization
         [3]. While it performs comparably to state-of-the-art baselines like GraphConv when large
         labeled datasets are available, it provides a distinct advantage when there is insufficient
         labeled data [4].
  c33 : The SMILES Transformer was developed to address the limitations of conventional, rule-based
         molecular fingerprints, which map molecules to a sparse discrete space and perform poorly
         when used with shallow prediction models or small datasets [3]. The SMILES Transformer is
         particularly effective in small-data settings, where pre-training facilitates good
         generalization [3]. It is especially useful when there is not enough labeled data available
         [4]. When large amounts of labeled data are available, the SMILES Transformer performs
         comparably to other state-of-the-art baseline models [2].

  QUESTION : Two different remedies for the fragility of SMILES appear in this corpus. What are
             they, and how do they differ in approach?
  EXPECTED : SELFIES changes the representation itself so every string is a valid molecule, while

             by unsupervised pretraining.

         However, it discusses two methods for addressing limitations or improving the utility of
         SMILES-based representations: 1. **Data Augmentation (Enumeration):** One approach to
         increasing the variety of data provided to a model is to generate multiple equivalent
         SMILES strings for a single molecule [1!]. By creating up to nine equivalent SMILES for
         each input molecule, the model is trained on a more diverse set of representations [1!].
         This technique, which involves using a set of different SMILES for the same molecule, is
         described as a key method for improving text-based molecular representations and
         restricting the latent space [8!]. 2. **Unsupervised Pre-training:** Another approach
         involves using a Transformer-based architecture to learn molecular fingerprints through
         unsupervised pre-training on a large corpus of SMILES [10!]. This method is designed to
         address the poor performance of rule-based algorithms in small-data settings or with
         shallow prediction models [10!]. By pre-training a sequence-to-sequence language model, the
         system learns to create representations that facilitate better generalization [10!].
         **Differences in Approach:** * **Data Augmentation** focuses on expanding the training data
         by creating multiple valid textual variations of the same molecule to improve the model's
         robustness during training [1, 8!]. * **Unsupervised Pre-training** focuses on the
         architecture and learning process itself, using a large, external corpus to teach the model
         how to interpret SMILES strings before it is applied to specific downstream tasks [10!].
         The corpus does not explicitly compare these two methods as "remedies for fragility," nor
         does it provide a direct comparison of their effectiveness against one another.
      why: gave SMILES enumeration/augmentation rather than SELFIES and the pretrained SMILES
           transformer
  c33 : The provided corpus does not explicitly use the term "fragility of SMILES." However, it
         describes two different approaches related to improving or handling SMILES representations:
         * **Data Augmentation (Generating Equivalent SMILES):** One approach involves increasing
         the variety of data provided to the model by generating up to nine equivalent SMILES for
         each input molecule [1!]. During training, one of the ten resulting SMILES is randomly
         selected to construct the input sequence [1!]. This method is used to increase the variety
         of data provided to the model [1!]. Another source notes that using a set of different
         SMILES for the same molecule can be used to "restrict the latent space" and is a key to
         improving text-based molecular representations [8!]. * **Unsupervised Pre-training:** A
         second approach, presented by the "SMILES Transformer," involves learning molecular
         fingerprints through unsupervised pre-training of a sequence-to-sequence language model
         using a "huge corpus of SMILES" [10!]. This method is intended to address the poor
         performance of rule-based algorithms in shallow prediction models or small datasets [10!].

Q119 cross_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts, [G] cited   support 1/2
  QUESTION : Unsupervised machine learning has been used to choose which elements to combine before
             any synthesis is attempted. What does the choice of elements determine, and why does it
             matter?
  EXPECTED : The elements chosen delimit the range of compositions, structures and therefore
             properties that can arise, so element selection bounds the possible outcomes of the
             synthetic chemistry before it begins.
  c26 : The choice of elements to combine is a foundational decision in synthetic chemistry [2].
         This selection determines the range of compositions and structures that can be formed,
         which in turn dictates the properties that can arise [1]. Because the choice of elements
         defines the "phase field" (the specific combination of elements being explored), it sets
         the boundaries for the outcomes of a study [1]. This choice matters for several reasons: *
         **Determining Outcomes:** The elemental components of a phase field determine the
         likelihood of discovering a new crystalline material [1]. * **Resource Allocation:**
         Because the choice of elements delimits the possible outcomes, it is a consequential
         decision that determines the subsequent investment of computational and experimental effort
         [2]. * **Complexity:** It is difficult for researchers to manage the large number of
         potential phase fields and the complex factors—such as kinetic or thermodynamic stability
         and diverse bonding interactions—that underlie the stability of compounds [5]. *
         **Strategic Prioritization:** By using unsupervised machine learning to learn the interplay
         between the chemical characteristics of elements in known, synthetically accessible
         crystalline solids, researchers can rank candidate phase fields [2]. This allows them to
         prioritize chemistries that are attractive (e.g., avoiding toxic or scarce elements) while
         respecting the criteria used to select them [9]. In summary, elements determine
         compositions [G] and structures [G], which ultimately determine the properties [G] of the
         resulting materials.
  c33 : The choice of elements to combine (referred to as a "phase field") determines the range of
         compositions and structures that can be attained, which in turn dictates the properties
         that can arise from the resulting materials [1]. This choice is significant for several
         reasons: * **It sets the boundaries for discovery:** The selection of elements delimits the
         possible outcomes of synthetic chemistry [1]. Because the choice of a phase field defines
         the bounds of attainable structures and compositions, it is considered a decisive factor in
         determining the outcomes of a study [3]. * **It impacts resource allocation:** Selecting
         which elements to combine is a foundational choice in synthetic chemistry that is
         consequential because it determines the amount of computational and experimental effort
         subsequently invested [2]. * **It involves managing complexity:** Researchers must navigate
         a vast number of potential phase fields, making it difficult to hold the necessary prior
         examples in mind when deciding which combinations to investigate [5!]. Machine learning can
         support human decision-making by identifying and prioritizing chemistries for experimental
         exploration [2].

         though the specific nature of this validity differs between the two contexts. **The Shared

         latent space or the final output correspond to valid, realistic structures [1, 6!]. *
         **Molecular VAE:** For molecular VAEs, the difficulty is ensuring that all points in the
         latent space correspond to valid molecules [6!]. * **Inorganic-compound VAE:** For
         inorganic material generation, the difficulty is that standard generative models often fail
         to produce compounds that are considered "realistic" or valid according to expert rules [1,

         is a desirable property for any application of VAEs in chemistry [6!]. *

         **Architecture:** It uses a "Binded-VAE" (BVAE) model, which splits the learning process
         into two chained VAEs: a "Mask-VAE" that generates a binary mask (indicating component
         presence) and a "Ratio-VAE" (a Conditional VAE) that generates the specific ratios of those
         components [2, 3, 8!]. * **Evaluation:** It employs a novel "Expert Rules Agreement" (ERA)

         because traditional validation processes like FID and Inception scores are tailored for
         images and are insufficient for this context [5!].

         the inorganic-compound VAE. Regarding the inorganic-compound VAE: * **Difficulty:** The
         primary difficulty is generating compounds that are realistic, given that the data is
         characterized by discrete features and severe sparsity [3]. Standard VAEs fail to generate
         compounds with a few non-zero components and adequate ratios because of this sparsity [3].
         Furthermore, traditional validation processes like FID and Inception scores are tailored
         for images and cannot be used directly for this context [3]. * **Handling:** The authors

         binary vector indicating component presence) and the component ratios [3]. They also
         propose three new metrics tailored to compound generation: an adapted FID (FID*), the KL

Q132 multi_hop   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts, [G] cited   support 1/2
  QUESTION : The convex hull is used as the stability criterion in several papers here. What does a
             structure lying on the hull mean, and which two efforts report hull results at very
             different scales?
  EXPECTED : Lying on the convex hull means the structure does not decompose into any combination of
             competing phases, so it is thermodynamically stable. GNoME reports 381,000 entries on
             the updated hull, while the active-learning alloy search reports hulls for three
             specific binary and ternary systems.
  c26 : A structure lying on the convex hull is considered thermodynamically stable [1]. It
         represents a material that is energetically favorable compared to competing phases, which
         are structures made of the same atomic constituents but in different proportions or spatial
         arrangements [1]. When a structure lies on the convex hull, it means it has the lowest
         formation energy in the phase diagram [2]. Conversely, structures with higher formation
         energies than the convex hull are considered unstable or metastable, as they would
         decompose into their closest stable neighbors on the hull [2]. The convex hull essentially
         represents the free energy of an alloy at zero temperature [4]. The corpus highlights two
         efforts that report results at significantly different scales regarding the convex hull: *
         **MatterGen:** This model assesses stability by performing DFT calculations on 1,024
         generated structures [10]. It evaluates these against two different references: the
         Materials Project (MP) convex hull and a combined AlexMP-ICSD hull [10]. * **Ab initio
         RSS:** This approach is described as the most comprehensive for defining the true convex
         hull in a target chemical system, but it is noted for its high computational cost, where
         exploring even a ternary compound can require hundreds of thousands of DFT relaxations [7].
         The convex hull is used to determine phase stability [4][G].
  c33 : A structure lying on the convex hull is considered thermodynamically stable [1]. It
         represents the lowest formation energy for a given composition compared to all alternative
         competing phases and linear combinations of phases [6]. If a structure lies on the convex
         hull, it is energetically favorable compared to competing phases built from the same atomic
         constituents in different proportions or spatial arrangements [1]. Conversely, structures
         with energies above the convex hull are considered unstable or metastable [4, 9!].
         Regarding efforts reporting hull results at different scales, the provided documents
         describe the following: * **The Materials Project (MP) database:** This is a large-scale
         reference used for defining convex hulls, containing hundreds of thousands of structures
         [1, 10!]. For example, one study references a combined "AlexMP-ICSD" hull that includes
         850,384 ordered structures and 117,652 disordered structures [10!]. * **Specific system
         explorations:** In contrast, other efforts focus on specific, smaller chemical systems. For
         instance, one study notes that exploring even a single ternary compound (a system with
         three elements) can require hundreds of thousands of DFT relaxations to define the "true
         convex hull" for that specific system [7!]. Another study discusses the "curse of
         dimensionality" for high N-ary materials (materials with N unique atom types), noting that
         these high-dimensional hulls are not well-represented in realistic datasets [3!].

Q133 cross_doc   c26 PARTIAL   |   c33 PARTIAL   graph 25 facts   support 0/3
  QUESTION : Why does the corpus treat generating a structure that sits on the convex hull and
             generating a synthesizable one as different problems?
  EXPECTED : Thermodynamic stability against the convex hull says nothing about whether a synthesis
             route exists; MatterGen's rediscovery of experimentally verified ICSD structures and
             the A-Lab's 63% success rate on computationally predicted targets are used to probe
             synthesizability separately.
  c26 : The corpus does not explicitly state that generating a structure on the convex hull and
         generating a synthesizable one are "different problems" in a categorical sense; rather, it
         treats the convex hull as a computational proxy or heuristic for synthesizability, while
         acknowledging the limitations of this approach. The relationship between these two concepts
         is described as follows: * **The convex hull as a heuristic:** Stability is considered a
         strong indicator of synthesizability [4]. Consequently, computational methods use the
         energy above the convex hull as a signal to estimate whether a material might be stable—and
         thus potentially synthesizable—at ambient temperature and pressure [9]. * **Limitations of
         computational evidence:** The corpus notes that it is not practically possible to
         conclusively determine that a material is synthesizable using only theoretical and
         computational evidence [9]. Even when a structure is found on the convex hull, researchers
         acknowledge limitations in their confidence regarding synthesizability, noting that such
         confidence is restricted to the specific level of theory and computation used [1!]. *
         **Epistemic uncertainty:** There is inherent epistemic uncertainty in using the convex hull
         to define stability because many materials remain unknown and are not represented on the
         convex hull [4]. * **Human evaluation:** Because computational metrics alone are
         insufficient to guarantee synthesizability, researchers sometimes employ human-assisted
         evaluation of predicted structures to identify failure modes that computational metrics
         might miss [1, 9!]. In summary, the corpus treats the convex hull as a computational tool
         to guide the search for synthesizable materials [9!], but it does not equate the two, as
         the convex hull is a theoretical construct subject to epistemic uncertainty [4!] and
         computational limitations [1, 9!].

Q140 cross_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts, [G] cited   support 1/2
  QUESTION : What is a protostructure in the Wyckoff-based description of crystals, and why is it
             useful for generative modelling?
  EXPECTED : A protostructure is the symmetry-level description of a crystal — space group plus
             occupied Wyckoff sites and species — without explicit coordinates, so a model can
             generate symmetry directly and leave the coordinates to relaxation.
  c26 : A protostructure is a representation of a crystal structure that assigns specific chemical
         elements to Wyckoff positions while leaving the degrees of freedom for those positions
         unspecified [3]. It is derived from the concept of a "prototype," which combines a space
         group with the occupation of Wyckoff positions by unspecified but distinct elements [3!].
         In the context of a Wyckoff-based description, a protostructure is represented as a tuple
         involving the space group and the occupancy of Wyckoff positions [4]. These positions are
         partitioned into two categories: * **Constrained positions:** These have no degrees of
         freedom, meaning an atom occupying the position is limited to a fixed point in space [2].
         They can be occupied by either 0 or 1 atom [1]. * **Unconstrained positions:** These have
         degrees of freedom, meaning an atom can be positioned anywhere on a line, in a plane, or in
         a volume [2]. In the modeling described, these can be occupied by a maximum of $P$ atoms of
         each type [6]. **Utility for Generative Modeling:** Protostructures are useful for
         generative modeling for several reasons: * **Efficiency:** By encoding symmetry in a
         protostructure description, the model avoids specifying exact atomic coordinates initially
         [4]. This allows for an initial identification of candidate crystal structures likely to be
         stable, which avoids wasting computational resources on exact coordinate calculations for
         all possible materials [4]. * **Symmetry Preservation:** This approach helps avoid the risk
         of generating degenerate materials or structures that fall outside of the symmetry
         proximity, which can occur when dealing with the infinite space of continuous coordinates
         [4]. * **Targeting Interesting Materials:** Since high-symmetry materials are generally the
         most interesting to explore, this method avoids the inefficiency of generating large sets
         of low-symmetry materials [4]. * **Pipeline Integration:** Protostructures can be realized
         into full crystal structures by semi-randomly assigning values to the degrees of freedom of
         the Wyckoff positions and then performing constrained relaxation to minimize energy while
         retaining the defined symmetries [8]. The corpus does not explicitly state why
         protostructures are useful for generative modeling beyond the efficiency and
         symmetry-related benefits listed above. While the relationships note that crystals are
         defined by unit cells and contain atoms [G], the provided text focuses on the
         protostructure as a way to represent these components without specifying exact coordinates
         [3, 4!].
  c33 : A protostructure is a representation of a crystal where specific chemical elements are
         assigned to Wyckoff positions, but the degrees of freedom for those positions remain
         unspecified [3]. It is derived from the concept of a "prototype," which combines a space
         group with the occupation of Wyckoff positions by unspecified but distinct elements [3!].
         In the context of the WyckoffDiff model, a protostructure is represented as a tuple
         involving a space group $s$ and the occupancy of Wyckoff positions [6]. These positions are
         partitioned into two types: * **Constrained positions:** These are limited to a fixed point
         in space and can be occupied by either 0 or 1 atom [1]. * **Unconstrained positions:**
         These allow atoms to be positioned anywhere on a line, in a plane, or in a volume [2]. In
         the model, these are represented by the number of atoms of each type occupying the
         position, with a maximum of $P$ atoms of each type allowed per position [6]. **Utility for
         Generative Modelling** The use of protostructures in generative modeling is beneficial for
         several reasons: * **Efficiency:** It allows for an initial identification of candidate
         crystal structures that are likely to be stable based solely on symmetry descriptions [4].
         This avoids the computational expense of calculating exact atomic coordinates for all
         possible materials [4]. * **Symmetry Preservation:** By encoding material symmetry in the
         protostructure, the model maintains key structural information without needing to specify
         exact atomic coordinates [4]. * **Avoiding Degeneracy:** It helps avoid the risk of
         generating degenerate materials or structures that fall outside of the symmetry proximity,
         which can occur when working within the infinite space of continuous coordinates [4]. *
         **Targeting High-Symmetry Materials:** Because high-symmetry materials are generally the
         most interesting to explore, using protostructures avoids the inefficiency of generating
         large sets of low-symmetry materials [4].

         efficiency and accuracy [9]. * **Locality and Additivity:** Many empirical potentials are

  QUESTION : What makes bandgap a recurring screening target across the photovoltaic and
             optoelectronic work in this corpus?
  EXPECTED : Bandgap determines which part of the solar spectrum a material absorbs, so it is the
             primary screen for photovoltaic candidates — used both to select lead-free HOIPs with
             visible-region direct bandgaps and as a conditioning property for generative and
             reinforcement-learning models.
  c26 : Bandgap is a recurring screening target in photovoltaic and optoelectronic research for the
         following reasons: * **Performance Optimization:** Bandgap is a critical parameter for
         optimizing solar cells. For instance, tandem solar cells utilize multiple absorber layers
         with different bandgaps to reduce thermalization losses and increase power conversion
         efficiency (PCE) [6]. * **Trade-off Management:** Optimizing materials for multiple
         objectives is challenging because improving one property often compromises another;
         specifically, decreasing the bandgap of a light-absorbing material in a photovoltaic cell
         increases photocurrent but decreases voltage [9]. * **Technological Application:** Wide
         bandgap materials (>2 eV) are essential for developing new optoelectronic devices capable
         of operating under blue and ultraviolet light [1]. * **Material Discovery and Screening:**
         Researchers use machine learning and computational methods to predict and screen for
         materials with specific bandgaps to meet technological requirements [1, 7!]. This is
         supported by various computational models, such as density functional theory, which is used
         to calculate bandgaps [G], and machine learning models (e.g., CGCNN, MEGNet, AFLOW-ML,
         JARVIS-ML), which are used to predict them [G].
  c33 : Based on the provided documents, bandgap is a recurring screening target for the following
         reasons: * **Optoelectronic Device Development:** Wide bandgap materials (>2 eV) are
         targeted because they are believed to be essential for developing new optoelectronic
         devices capable of operating under blue and ultraviolet light [1]. * **Tandem Solar Cell
         Efficiency:** Tandem solar cells use multiple absorber layers with different bandgaps to
         reduce thermalization losses and increase power conversion efficiency (PCE) compared to
         single-junction cells [6]. * **Trade-off Management:** Bandgap is a critical variable in
         multiobjective optimization because it involves trade-offs; for instance, decreasing the
         bandgap of a light-absorbing material in a photovoltaic cell typically increases
         photocurrent but decreases voltage [9]. * **Material Discovery and Screening:** Researchers
         use machine learning to predict bandgaps for unexplored materials to identify promising
         candidates for solar cells [7]. For example, screening processes have been used to select
         stable lead-free hybrid organic-inorganic perovskites (HOIPs) that possess "proper"
         bandgaps [7]. The corpus does not explicitly explain why bandgap is a screening target
         beyond these specific functional and optimization contexts.

Q158 single_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts, [G] cited   support 1/1
  QUESTION : What structural validity threshold is applied to generated crystal structures, and what
             is the compositional validity criterion?
  EXPECTED : A structure is valid if its shortest atomic distance exceeds 0.5 Å, a deliberately
             lenient standard; composition validity requires charge neutrality.
  c26 : The structural validity threshold applied to generated crystal structures is that all
         pairwise atomic distances must exceed 0.5 Å [4]. Some sources describe this as a
         requirement that no two atoms are closer than 0.5 Å apart [2]. The compositional validity
         criterion is that the generated structure must have an overall neutral charge [1]. This is
         determined using the SMACT heuristic system [2][G].
  c33 : The structural validity threshold and compositional validity criterion are defined as
         follows: * **Structural Validity:** A generated crystal structure is considered
         structurally valid if all pairwise atomic distances are greater than 0.5 Å [1, 2, 4, 5, 6,
         9!]. One source notes that this is a "lenient standard" [6!]. Another source defines it as
         the percentage of generated structures where the shortest distance between any two atoms is
         greater than 0.5 Å [9!]. * **Compositional Validity:** A structure is considered
         compositionally valid if it has an overall neutral charge [1, 4, 5, 6!]. This is determined
         using SMACT [1, 2, 4, 5, 6!]. One source notes that this criterion is "overly stringent"
         because the composition validity of the training set itself is only around 90% [6!].

Q163 single_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts   support 1/1
  QUESTION : What is the stated purpose of the asymmetric unit in a symmetry-preserving crystal
             diffusion model?
  EXPECTED : It is the smallest subset of the crystal that can generate the whole crystal through
             symmetry transformations, so learning it plus the transformations reproduces the full
             structure.
  c26 : The asymmetric unit serves several purposes within a symmetry-preserving crystal diffusion
         model: * **Compact Representation:** It provides a compact, complete representation of the
         crystal structure by containing no redundancy [5]. It is defined as a small part of the
         unit cell that contains no symmetry but can be used to generate the entire unit cell by
         applying the space group's symmetry transformations [2]. * **Symmetry Preservation:**
         Because the diffusion and denoising processes operate exclusively on the asymmetric unit,
         the symmetry of the crystal is fully preserved [4]. * **Computational Efficiency:** Using
         the asymmetric unit significantly improves computational performance compared to using the
         conventional unit cell. It allows for larger maximum batch sizes (8192 vs. 512), requires
         less memory (3.6 GB vs. 31 GB for a batch size of 512), and reduces training time per epoch
         (27 seconds vs. 52 seconds) [10!]. * **Geometric Information Recovery:** While using only
         the asymmetric unit can result in a loss of geometric information, accessing site symmetry
         information helps recover this data, which in turn aids in predicting target properties
         [1].
  c33 : The stated purposes of the asymmetric unit in the context of the symmetry-preserving
         crystal diffusion model are as follows: * **Compact Representation:** The asymmetric unit
         serves as a "small part of the unit cell" that contains no symmetry but can be used to
         generate the entire unit cell by applying the space group's symmetry transformations [2!].
         It is a "maximal subset of the unit cell with no redundancy" [7!]. Leveraging this allows
         for a "much more compact, yet complete representation" of the crystal [5!]. * **Generative
         Process:** The diffusion and denoising processes operate exclusively on the asymmetric
         unit, which ensures that the symmetry of the crystal is fully preserved [4, 5!]. *
         **Unfolding/Generation:** The asymmetric unit acts as an "unconstrained space" where atoms
         can be generated; once the site symmetries are generated and the atomic positions are made
         consistent with them, the asymmetric unit can be "unfolded" to create a symmetric crystal
         [7!]. During sampling, the asymmetric unit is replicated to obtain the final unit cell
         [3!]. * **Computational Efficiency:** Using the asymmetric unit instead of the conventional
         unit cell allows for larger batch sizes (8192 vs 512), reduced memory usage (3.6 GB vs 31
         GB for a batch size of 512), and faster training epochs (27 seconds vs 52 seconds) [10!].

         the model is provided with the chemical composition of the material beforehand: * **Crystal
         Structure Prediction (CSP):** In this setting, the user provides the chemical composition,
         which includes the number of constituent atoms and their elemental types [8]. The
         generative model is then tasked with predicting the structural parameters (such as the
         crystal structure) based on this given composition [3]. * **De Novo Generation (DNG):** In
         this setting, the chemical composition (atom types) is not pre-specified [3]. The
         generative model is tasked with producing both the composition and the crystal structure
         simultaneously [8]. While both tasks are used in the context of inorganic materials design
         [G], the fundamental distinction lies in the input requirements: CSP requires the
         composition as an input [8!], whereas DNG requires the model to generate the composition
         itself [3, 8!].

  c33 : The two tasks differ primarily in the information provided to the model and the scope of
         what the model is required to predict: * **Crystal Structure Prediction (CSP):** In this
         setting, the user provides the composition, which includes the number of constituent atoms
         and their specific elemental types [8]. The model is then tasked with predicting the
         crystal structure based on this provided information [8]. * **De Novo Generation (DNG):**
         In this setting, the chemical composition (atom types) is not pre-specified [3]. The model
         is tasked with simultaneously predicting both the crystal structure (structural parameters)
         and the composition (atom types) [3]. The provided documents do not explicitly detail how

         both tasks are evaluated using proxy metrics [2] and that DNG performance is specifically

Q168 cross_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts, [G] cited   support 1/2
  QUESTION : What distinguishes an invariant model from an equivariant one for interatomic
             potentials, and which architecture in this corpus argues the distinction matters for
             accuracy?
  EXPECTED : Invariant models act only on scalars such as distances, whereas equivariant models
             propagate geometric tensors that transform with the system; NequIP argues this gives a
             more information-rich and faithful representation of atomic environments.
  c26 : The distinction between invariant and equivariant models for interatomic potentials lies in
         how they process geometric information and represent atomic features: * **Invariant
         Models:** These architectures are designed to be invariant with respect to translations,
         rotations, and permutations of atoms [6]. They typically rely on scalar-valued features and
         invariant convolutions, which operate on geometric data such as radial distances or angles
         [6, 9!]. * **Equivariant Models:** These architectures use equivariant convolutions, which
         ensure that if a feature at a given layer is transformed (e.g., by rotation or parity), the
         output of the convolution transforms accordingly [1]. Unlike invariant models that act only
         on scalars, equivariant models (such as NequIP) utilize geometric tensors—including
         scalars, vectors, and higher-order tensors—as internal features [8]. This allows them to
         represent tensor properties and operations (like dot products or cross products) more
         generally [5]. The architecture that argues this distinction is critical for accuracy is
         **NequIP** (Neural Equivariant Interatomic Potential) [2]. NequIP is built on
         E(3)-equivariant graph neural networks [2][G]. The corpus notes that NequIP's superior
         performance, including state-of-the-art accuracy and data efficiency, is specifically
         attributed to the use of equivariant representations instead of the more common invariant
         ones [2, 10!]. Experiments comparing NequIP to conventional invariant GNN-IPs (by disabling
         interactions involving higher-order tensors) demonstrated that the equivariant networks
         significantly outperformed the invariant ones across various training set sizes [10].
  c33 : The distinction between invariant and equivariant models for interatomic potentials lies in
         how they process geometric information and represent physical properties: * **Invariant
         Models:** These models are designed to be invariant with respect to translations,
         rotations, and permutations of atoms [6]. They typically operate on scalar features (such
         as radial distances or angles) and use invariant convolutions [6, 9, 10!]. In these
         architectures, the network acts only on scalars [9]. * **Equivariant Models:** These models
         use equivariant convolutions, which ensure that if a feature is transformed (e.g., by
         rotation or parity), the output of the convolution is transformed accordingly [1]. Unlike
         invariant models, equivariant models represent tensor properties (such as vector addition,
         dot products, and cross products) and use internal features that are tensors equivariant to
         rotation [5]. While the final output (e.g., total potential energy) remains invariant to
         rotation and translation, the network's internal features are geometric objects comprising
         a direct sum of irreducible representations of the SO(3) symmetry group [8]. The
         architecture identified in the corpus that argues this distinction is critical for accuracy
         is **NequIP** (Neural Equivariant Interatomic Potential) [2]. NequIP demonstrates that the
         use of equivariant representations—specifically tensor features and equivariant
         convolutions—enables superior sample efficiency and accuracy compared to invariant
         representations [2]. The corpus notes that NequIP outperforms conventional invariant
         GNN-IPs (which involve only invariant convolutions over scalar features) across various
         training set sizes [10].

Q172 cross_doc   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts   support 1/2
  QUESTION : What does the corpus give as the reason that generated crystal structures still need
             density functional theory afterwards?
  EXPECTED : Generative models produce candidate geometries whose energies are unknown, so DFT
             relaxation and convex-hull evaluation are needed to establish whether the candidate is
             actually stable.
  c26 : The corpus identifies several reasons why generated crystal structures require density
         functional theory (DFT) analysis after generation: * **Verification of stability:** DFT
         calculations are necessary to determine if a generated composition is stable enough to be
         used in practice [1!]. Many generated structures are not inherently stable and require
         non-trivial DFT relaxations to be optimized [4!]. In some cases, models generate a large
         number of structures, but only a small fraction are found to be stable after DFT relaxation
         [6!]. * **Validation of physical validity:** A common issue in structure generation is that
         models may produce "random" structures that do not conform to fundamental crystallography
         principles [4!]. DFT is used to verify the stability of materials generated by methods like
         DiffCSP++ [4!]. * **Performance and quality assessment:** DFT is used to thoroughly analyze
         the quality of generated structures [2, 5!]. It serves as a standard for predicting and
         validating the thermodynamic, mechanical, and electronic properties of inorganic materials
         [10!]. * **Optimization and convergence:** DFT is used to perform local optimizations on
         generated structures. For example, comparisons between models like CrystalFlow and
         Cond-CDVAE use DFT to evaluate how close generated structures are to their local minima and
         how much relaxation is required to achieve stability [9!]. * **Ranking and prediction:** In
         blind tests of crystal-structure prediction, DFT is used as a method to rank predicted
         structures based on their likelihood [7!]. While DFT is the standard for these tasks, the
         corpus notes that it is computationally expensive, making it impossible to run on all data
         generated by models like CrystalGAN [1!]. Additionally, DFT's reliance on known structures
         presents a challenge when attempting to generate entirely new inorganic materials with
         unknown structures [10!].
  c33 : Based on the provided documents, generated crystal structures require density functional
         theory (DFT) for the following reasons: * **Verifying stability:** DFT calculations are
         necessary to determine if a generated composition is stable enough to be used in practice
         [1]. Many generated structures are not inherently stable and require non-trivial DFT
         relaxations to be optimized [4]. In some cases, models generate a large number of
         structures, but only a small fraction are found to be stable after DFT relaxation [6!]. *
         **Validating properties:** DFT is used as the standard for predicting and validating the
         electronic, mechanical, and thermodynamic properties of inorganic materials [10]. *
         **Assessing quality:** Detailed DFT calculations are used to analyze the quality of
         generated structures [2]. * **Refining structures:** DFT local optimizations are used to
         move generated structures closer to their local minima, reducing the amount of relaxation
         required to achieve stability [9]. The corpus does not address whether there are any
         alternative methods to DFT that could serve these same functions.

  QUESTION : A crystal generative model reports both a match rate on MP-20 and a stable-unique-novel
             rate. Why can a model score well on one and badly on the other?
  EXPECTED : Match rate measures reconstruction of known held-out structures, which rewards fidelity
             to the training distribution, whereas the S.U.N. rate rewards producing stable
             structures that are not already known — so a faithful reconstructor can be an
             unremarkable discoverer.
  c26 : The provided corpus does not explicitly explain why a model might score well on Match Rate
         (MR) while performing poorly on the Stable-Unique-Novel (SUN) rate. However, the excerpts
         do describe the distinct nature of these two evaluation tasks and the challenges associated
         with generating stable structures, which provides context for why performance may differ
         between them. **Differences in Evaluation Tasks** * **Crystal Structure Prediction (CSP)
         and Match Rate:** The Match Rate (MR) is a metric used to evaluate Crystal Structure
         Prediction (CSP) performance [1, 2, 5!]. It measures how well a model can reconstruct a
         specific ground truth structure from a test set [1, 2!]. This task focuses on the model's
         ability to predict a known, specific structure given a composition or other conditioning
         [7!]. * **De Novo Generation (DNG) and SUN Rate:** The SUN rate evaluates a model's ability
         to generate *new* materials [3, 10!]. A structure is considered stable if its energy above
         the convex hull is negative [3, 10!], unique if it is distinct from other generated
         structures, and novel if it does not appear in the training set [3, 10!]. This task focuses
         on the generation of thermodynamically stable, diverse, and previously unseen materials
         rather than the reconstruction of known ones [10]. **Challenges in Generating Stable
         Structures** * **Relaxation and Stability:** The SUN rate is calculated after geometric
         optimization (relaxation) of the generated structures [3]. Some models, such as CDVAE and
         DiffCSP, are noted to generate a large number of structures with high numbers of unique
         elements per crystal (high N-ary), but these structures often fail to relax into stable
         configurations after density functional theory (DFT) calculations [4]. * **Model
         Performance:** Because the SUN rate depends on the thermodynamic stability of the generated
         structures after relaxation [3, 4!], a model might be capable of generating structures that
         match known test set data (achieving a high Match Rate) but fail to produce structures that
         are thermodynamically stable or novel (resulting in a low SUN rate) [4!]. The corpus does
         not contain a direct comparison or analysis explaining the specific correlation or lack
         thereof between a model's Match Rate and its SUN rate.
      why: described CSP and DNG separately but declined to say why a model can win one and lose the
           other
  c33 : The provided documents do not explicitly explain the specific reasons why a model might
         score well on Match Rate (MR) while performing poorly on the Stable-Unique-Novel (SUN)
         rate, or vice versa. However, the corpus provides information on the distinct nature of
         these two tasks and their evaluation metrics: * **Different Objectives:** Crystal Structure
         Prediction (CSP) tasks, which use Match Rate (MR) and Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE) as
         metrics, evaluate a model's ability to predict a specific ground truth structure given a
         known composition [1, 2, 7!]. In contrast, De Novo Generation (DNG) tasks, which use the
         SUN rate, evaluate a model's ability to generate new, diverse, and thermodynamically stable
         materials [3, 6, 10!]. * **Stability vs. Generation:** One document notes that while some
         models generate a large number of high N-ary (number of unique elements) structures, they
         find relatively few stable ones after relaxation [4!]. This implies that a model's ability
         to generate structures does not guarantee that those structures will be thermodynamically
         stable (a requirement for the "S" in SUN) [4!]. * **Evaluation Methods:** The SUN rate is
         calculated after geometric optimization (pre-relaxation and DFT relaxation) [3!]. Match
         Rate is calculated by generating $k$ candidate structures and determining if any match the
         ground truth structure [1, 2!]. The corpus does not contain a direct comparison or analysis

         and efficient replacements for traditional methods, such as quantum mechanical calculations
         or empirical interatomic potentials. For example, they are designed to achieve a balance
         where they provide the accuracy of density functional theory (DFT) while maintaining the

         and versatility, while simultaneously being orders of magnitude less costly than quantum
         mechanical methods [3]. **As a component of a larger discovery pipeline** MLIPs are

         create interatomic potentials that are more accurate and versatile than conventional
         potentials, while being significantly less costly than quantum mechanical methods [3!].
         They are viewed as versatile descriptions of the potential energy surface (PES) that aim to
         achieve the accuracy of density functional theory (DFT) while maintaining the efficiency of
         empirical interatomic potentials [7]. * **As a component of a larger discovery pipeline:**
         MLIPs can be used to generate new training data for further modeling. By using a smooth
         representation of the full configurational space, ML models can generate data that serves
         as a starting point for larger networks or other machine learning architectures [2].
         Additionally, local machine learning descriptions can be combined with many-body expansion
         methods to apply these potentials to larger systems, such as water clusters [1].

  QUESTION : How does the corpus connect the choice of elements to combine with the eventual
             probability of discovering a new crystalline material?
  EXPECTED : Element selection determines the range of accessible compositions and structures and
             therefore properties, so the elemental components of a phase field set the likelihood
             of finding a new crystalline material before any synthesis begins.
  c26 : The corpus connects the choice of elements to the discovery of new crystalline materials in
         the following ways: * **Delimiting outcomes:** The selection of elements to combine is a
         foundational decision in synthetic chemistry because it defines the "phase field" [2]. This
         choice sets the bounds for the range of compositions and structures that can be attained,
         which in turn determines the likelihood of discovering a new crystalline material [2]. *
         **Determining properties:** The elemental components of a phase field determine the
         properties that can arise in the resulting materials [2]. Elements determine the
         compositions and structures of materials [G], and these choices delimit the outcomes of
         synthetic chemistry [G]. * **Guiding discovery via machine learning:** Researchers can use
         unsupervised machine learning to learn the interplay between chemical characteristics of
         elements in combinations that have already yielded synthetically accessible crystalline
         inorganic solids [1]. By connecting the latent space of a Variational Autoencoder (VAE) to
         chemical space, researchers can rank candidate phase fields [1]. This ranking quantifies
         the likelihood that specific element combinations will lead to new isolated compounds,
         helping researchers prioritize which chemistries to explore experimentally [10]. *
         **Complexity of factors:** While the choice of elements is decisive, the factors underlying
         the stability of compounds—whether kinetic or thermodynamic—are complex and reflect diverse
         bonding interactions between the constituent elements [7]. Researchers often find it
         difficult to hold the thousands of prior examples necessary to make these decisions
         manually [7!].
  c33 : The corpus explains the connection between the choice of elements and the discovery of new
         crystalline materials in the following ways: * **The foundational role of element
         selection:** The selection of elements to combine—referred to as a "phase field"—is a
         foundational decision in synthetic chemistry [1]. This choice is decisive because it
         delimits the range of possible compositions and structures that can be attained, which in
         turn determines the likelihood of discovering a new crystalline material [2]. * **The
         difficulty of the choice:** Because of the vast scale of available options and the
         diversity of bonding and structure types, it is difficult for researchers to evaluate the
         large number of unexplored phase fields [1]. * **Machine learning as a guide:** To address
         this, machine learning (specifically a Variational Autoencoder, or VAE) can be used to
         learn the interplay between the chemical characteristics of elements in combinations that
         have already yielded synthetically accessible crystalline inorganic solids [1]. By
         connecting the latent space of the VAE to chemical space, the model can rank candidate
         phase fields based on patterns of similarity to known, successful chemistries [1]. *
         **Building researcher trust:** This numerical ranking helps researchers prioritize which
         chemistries to investigate experimentally [10]. The VAE quantifies the likelihood that
         specific element combinations will lead to new isolated compounds, and the viability of
         these highly scored combinations helps build researcher trust in the tool [10]. *
         **Modifying probability for novelty:** In some computational approaches, probabilistic
         models are adjusted to prioritize discovery [4!]. For example, by modifying model
         parameters or implementation details (such as computing probabilities only when
         compositions differ), researchers can move away from known sets of stable crystals and give
         priority to novel discovery rather than favoring combinations that are more likely to be
         found in existing datasets [4].

  c33 : Based on the provided documents, the approaches to achieving diversity differ as follows:
         **Reward-based samplers (GFlowNets)** * **Sampling proportional to reward:** GFlowNets

         specifically advantageous when the reward function is highly multimodal, as it allows the

  QUESTION : The corpus contains both a transformer that consumes crystal strings and one that
             consumes crystal graphs. What does each need the crystal to be reduced to before the
             model sees it?
  EXPECTED : MatterGPT needs the crystal encoded as a SLICES string of characters, while Matformer
             and ComFormer need it reduced to a periodic graph with lattice-aware geometric
             descriptors.
  c26 : Based on the provided documents, the requirements for crystal representation before being
         consumed by transformer models are as follows: **Crystal Strings** * **SLICES:** To address
         the limitations of existing models in capturing complex spatial information, the SLICES
         (Simplified LineInput Crystal-Encoding System) approach encodes crystal structures into
         simple, invertible, and invariant strings [3]. * **CrystalFormer:** This model represents
         the number of atoms in a unit cell as the length of non-padding atoms within a crystal
         string representation [4]. **Crystal Graphs** * **Periodic Invariance:** For models like
         Matformer, it is necessary to maintain periodic invariance. Breaking this invariance
         results in different crystal graphs for the same crystal, which is a method employed by the
         Graphormer model [7]. * **Geometric Completeness:** For ComFormer (specifically
         iComFormer), the input must be a geometrically complete SE(3) invariant crystal graph [8].
         For eComFormer, the input is an SO(3) equivariant crystal graph [8]. * **Mapping:** Crystal
         graphs are used to represent atoms [G] and are processed by convolution functions [G].
         These graphs are constructed using nearest neighbors [G] and self-connecting edges [G].
  c33 : Based on the provided documents, the requirements for crystal representation before being
         processed by these models are as follows: **Transformers consuming crystal strings** *
         **SLICES:** For the MatterGPT model, crystals are encoded into "simple strings" using the
         Simplified LineInput Crystal-Encoding System (SLICES), which is described as an invertible
         and invariant "crystal language" [3!]. * **CrystalFormer:** This model uses a "crystal
         string representation," where the length of the non-padding atoms in the string corresponds
         to the number of atoms in the unit cell [4!]. **Transformers consuming crystal graphs** *
         **ComFormer:** This model utilizes "SE(3) invariant" or "SO(3) equivariant" crystal graphs
         [5, 8!]. These graphs are designed to map different unit cell structures of the same
         crystal to the same invariant or equivariant graph representation [5!]. * **Matformer:**
         This model aims to integrate "periodic invariance" into its graph construction to avoid
         breaking the invariance, which would otherwise result in different crystal graphs for the
         same crystal [7!]. * **Graphormer:** This model builds a fully connected graph after using
         a radius to include atoms of interest in nearby cells, a method that the corpus notes
         breaks periodic invariance [7!]. The corpus does not provide information regarding the
         specific reduction requirements for other transformer models that might consume crystal
         graphs or strings beyond those mentioned above.

Q186 multi_hop   c26 CORRECT   |   c33 CORRECT   graph 25 facts   support 2/2
  QUESTION : How does the reporting bias in synthesis literature propagate into a generative model,
             and what two remedies does this corpus offer?
  EXPECTED : Datasets record only successful or optimal syntheses, so a model trained on them never
             sees the boundary between what works and what does not; the remedies offered are
             synthetically generating reactions from a VAE latent space, and treating failed
             experiments as training data in their own right.
  c26 : Based on the provided corpus, reporting bias propagates into generative models and machine
         learning systems in the following ways: * **Lack of negative examples:** Published
         literature often lacks negative examples (failed synthesis attempts), which limits the
         ability of models to learn which specific factors contribute to an experiment's success
         [3!]. * **Skewed data:** Researchers' biases influence the design of experiments, which
         combines with other biases to skew the reported data [8!]. * **Incomplete learning:**
         Machine learning techniques perform best when trained on a continuous range of inputs and
         outputs; because biased datasets do not provide this, they hinder the model's ability to
         learn effectively [8!]. The corpus suggests the following two approaches as potential
         remedies for dataset issues, though it notes that there is no consensus on how to deal with
         bias [2!]: 1. **Generative techniques:** Generative models (such as MedGAN) can be used to
         generate realistic synthetic data, which has shown progress in fields facing similar
         dataset challenges [2]. 2. **NLP-based data curation:** NLP efforts can be used to collect
         and curate synthesis data directly from the literature to overcome the lack of
         well-structured, high-quality data available for autonomous systems [3].
  c33 : Based on the provided documents, reporting bias propagates into generative models and
         machine learning systems in the following ways: * **Influence on experimental design:**
         Researchers' biases influence the creation and design of experiments, which combines with
         other types of biases to skew the reported data [8!]. * **Lack of negative examples:**
         Because of inherent biases in published results, there is a lack of negative examples
         (failed synthesis attempts) in the data. This limits the ability of models to learn which
         specific factors contribute to an experiment's success [3!]. The corpus offers the
         following two remedies to address these issues: * **Natural Language Processing (NLP):**
         NLP efforts can be used to collect and curate synthesis data from relevant literature. This
         helps overcome the limited amount of well-structured, high-quality data available to be
         coupled with autonomous systems [3!]. * **Generative techniques:** The use of generative
         techniques to create realistic synthetic data has shown progress in other fields facing
         similar dataset issues and can be used to address the challenges posed by biased datasets
         [2].

  QUESTION : Which generative approaches in this corpus enforce crystallographic symmetry, and by
             what mechanism does each do it?
  EXPECTED : SymmCD via the asymmetric unit plus learned symmetry transformations, WyckoffDiff via a
             discrete Wyckoff representation, WyCryst via a Wyckoff-based representation with a
             property-directed VAE, and the space-group informed transformer by conditioning on the
             space group and filling Wyckoff positions.
  c26 : Based on the provided corpus, the following generative approaches enforce crystallographic
         symmetry through specific mechanisms: * **SymmCD:** This diffusion-based model explicitly
         incorporates crystallographic symmetry by decomposing crystals into two components: the
         asymmetric unit (the smallest subset of the crystal capable of generating the whole through
         symmetry transformations) and the symmetry transformations themselves [1]. It represents
         these transformations as binary matrices, which allows for information-sharing and
         generalization across different crystal and site symmetries [2]. * **MatterGen:** This
         model generates materials with a target symmetry by fine-tuning on space group labels [7].
         It is noted that generative models produce models with target symmetry [G]. * **Various
         Crystal-Structure Prediction (CSP) submissions:** In the context of the Sixth Blind Test of
         Organic Crystal-Structure Prediction, the majority of submissions explicitly imposed
         crystallographic symmetry by exploring a set of space groups, typically based on their
         frequency in the Cambridge Structural Database (CSD) [3]. Additionally, software utilities
         such as PLATON, PyMatGen, FINDSYM, and Spglib have been used to detect and enforce symmetry
         in the final structures generated by these methods [3]. * **CrystalGAN:** This model uses
         geometric constraints to control the quality of generated data [4]. It enforces these
         constraints to satisfy the geometric conditions of its scientific domain application, where
         a crystallographic structure is fully described by a local distribution determined by
         distances to all nearest neighbors of each atom [4]. The corpus also notes that while some
         models (such as string-based models using sequential tokenization like CIFs or SLICES) are
         well-suited for scaling to large datasets, they generally do not explicitly consider
         crystal symmetry [9]. Furthermore, existing crystal generation methods often fail to
         generate materials that display the symmetries of real-world crystals or simply replicate
         symmetry information from database examples [1].
  c33 : Based on the provided corpus, the following generative approaches enforce crystallographic
         symmetry: * **SymmCD:** This model explicitly incorporates crystallographic symmetry into
         the generative process by decomposing crystals into two components: the asymmetric unit and
         the symmetry transformations required to generate the full crystal from that unit [1]. It
         represents these symmetries as binary matrices, which allows for information-sharing and
         generalization across different crystal and site symmetries [2]. * **MatterGen:** This
         model enforces symmetry by fine-tuning on space group labels to generate materials with a
         target symmetry [7]. * **CrystalGAN:** This model enforces geometric constraints during its
         second step of generation to ensure the quality of the generated data [4]. These
         constraints are based on the local distribution of the crystallographic structure,
         specifically the distances to all nearest neighbors of each atom [4]. * **Methods in the
         Sixth Blind Test of Organic Crystal-Structure Prediction:** The majority of submissions in
         this test explicitly explored a set of space groups, typically chosen based on their
         frequency in the Cambridge Structural Database (CSD) [3]. Additionally, software utilities
         such as PLATON, PyMatGen, FINDSYM, and Spglib have been used to detect and enforce symmetry

  QUESTION : Which distinct crystal representations are used by the generative models in this
             corpus?
  EXPECTED : Lattice plus fractional coordinates (DiffCSP, CrystalFlow, FlowMM), Wyckoff-position
             protostructures (WyckoffDiff, WyCryst), SLICES strings (MatterGPT), text for a language
             model (FlowLLM), and image encodings such as PNG channels (xtal2png).
  c26 : Based on the provided corpus, generative models for crystals utilize several distinct types
         of representations: * **Composition-based representations:** These represent structures
         using simple stoichiometry or elemental substitutions (phase-fields) [2]. * **Graph-based
         representations:** These models are frequently combined with diffusion or flow techniques
         and equivariant message-passing networks to capture intrinsic crystal symmetries like
         permutation, rotation, and periodic translation [5, 10!]. * **String-based
         representations:** These models use sequential tokenization of crystal structures [3, 10!].
         Examples include: * **Standard Crystallographic Information Files (CIFs):** Used for
         sequential data processing [3, 10!]. * **SLICES (Simplified Line-Input Crystal-Encoding
         System):** An invertible and invariant "crystal language" designed to encode crystal
         structures into simple strings [8, 10!]. * **Prototype-based representations:** These
         define a structure by combining the space group with the Wyckoff positions occupied by
         distinct elements, without specifying the remaining degrees of freedom [6!]. A
         "protostructure" is a specific type of prototype where chemical elements are assigned to
         these Wyckoff positions [6!]. The corpus does not provide information on other potential
         representations beyond these categories.
      why: gave composition- and graph-based representations generically, not the specific five
  c33 : Based on the provided documents, the generative models for crystal structures are broadly
         categorized into two main types based on their data representation: * **Graph-based
         models:** These models are frequently combined with diffusion or flow techniques and
         equivariant message-passing networks [5]. This approach is effective for capturing
         intrinsic crystal symmetries, such as permutation, rotation, and periodic translation [5].
         * **String-based models:** These models utilize sequential tokenization of crystal
         structures [3]. Examples of these formats include: * **Standard Crystallographic
         Information Files (CIFs):** Used for sequential data processing [3]. * **SLICES (Simplified
         LineInput Crystal-Encoding System):** A representation designed to be invertible and
         invariant to Euclidean transformations, functioning as a "crystal language" analogous to
         SMILES for molecules [8]. Additionally, the corpus mentions other representations or
         concepts used in generative modeling: * **Composition/Stoichiometry:** Described as the
         simplest representation for structure, involving elemental substitutions [2]. *
         **Phase-fields:** Defined as elemental substitutions leading to distinct chemistries or
         physics [2]. * **Prototypes and Protostructures:** A prototype is defined as the
         combination of a space group and how Wyckoff positions are occupied by unspecified but
         distinct elements [6]. A protostructure refers to a prototype where specific chemical
         elements are assigned to the Wyckoff positions [6]. * **Atomistic features:** Used in some
         autoregressive models [7].

  QUESTION : Which sources of bias in materials machine learning are identified across this corpus?
  EXPECTED : Databases restricted to ordered crystalline materials, synthesis literature reporting
             only successful or optimal outcomes, novelty measured against less stringent reference
             sets, and training sets skewed toward well-studied chemistries.
  c26 : Based on the provided corpus, the following sources of bias in materials machine learning
         and data-driven research are identified: * **Publication Bias:** Data that is not essential
         for a publication is often left unpublished, which creates "publication bias" [9!]. This
         forces other researchers to repeat work to create balanced datasets, leading to a waste of
         resources [9!]. * **Data Bias:** It is noted that negative data is often as important as
         positive results for machine learning algorithms to prevent bias in the data [9!].
         Additionally, data bias can be difficult to quantify for users of data infrastructures [4,
         5!]. * **Underfitting:** The bias-variance trade-off is a balancing act between over- and
         underfitting [6, 8!]. Underfitting, which occurs when a model learns rules that are too
         general, is associated with bias [G]. * **Featurization:** The process of featurization is
         identified as a source that introduces bias [G]. * **Theoretical and Experimental
         Sources:** In the context of experiments or calculations, bias is defined as the offset of
         the average result from the ground truth [4, 5!]. This bias originates from approximations
         in theoretical models or experimental uncertainties, rather than from stochastic processes
         [4, 5!]. The corpus does not explicitly link "Bias Mitigation" techniques to the specific
         sources of bias mentioned above, though it notes that Bias Mitigation is a focus within
         machine learning [G].
      why: gave publication bias — related but not the four sources asked for (0/3 support docs)
  c33 : The provided corpus identifies several sources of bias relevant to materials science and
         machine learning: * **Theoretical and Experimental Sources:** In the context of experiments
         or calculations, bias is defined as the offset of an average result from the ground truth
         [4]. This type of bias originates from approximations in theoretical models or experimental
         uncertainties, rather than from stochastic processes in the experiment or calculation [4].
         * **Publication Bias:** This occurs when data that emerges as a side product of
         research—but is not essential for a publication—is left unpublished [9]. This creates a
         lack of balanced datasets, forcing other researchers to repeat work to generate the
         necessary data for machine learning applications [9]. * **Data Imbalance:** Negative data
         are often as important as positive results for machine learning algorithms to prevent bias
         in the data [9]. * **Database Limitations:** When using existing scientific literature
         databases for text mining, a source of bias is that the vast majority of publications are
         heavily skewed toward biomedicine and biochemistry, with only a small number of topics
         covering physics, organic chemistry, and materials science [7!]. The corpus also notes that
         the term "bias" as used in the context of experimental/computational results (offset from
         ground truth) should be disambiguated from the terminology commonly used in machine
         learning (such as the bias-variance trade-off) [4]. The corpus does not provide further
         details on specific algorithmic biases beyond the general mention of the bias-variance
         trade-off, which is controlled via cross-validation and dataset design [6].

  c26 : Based on the provided corpus, the following strategies for handling scarce training data
         are mentioned: * **Unsupervised Pre-training:** The SMILES Transformer addresses the issue
         of poor performance in small datasets by using unsupervised pre-training of a
         sequence-to-sequence language model on a large corpus of SMILES (a text representation
         system for molecules) [8!]. This pre-training facilitates good generalization in small-data
         settings [8!]. * **Multi-task Pre-training:** The DPA-2 model utilizes multi-task
         pre-training on a large corpus of datasets to improve generalizability to downstream tasks
         [10!]. This allows for "zero-shot generalization," where the model is tested directly on
         downstream tasks without needing further refinement data [10!]. * **Domain-specific
         Pre-training:** Models can be pre-trained on large, domain-specific text corpora before
         being applied to downstream tasks [5]. For example, MatSciBERT is pre-trained on a large
         corpus of materials science text [5], and MatBERT is pre-trained on a corpus of materials

         learning methods noted above. Additionally, it is established that machine learning models
         generally require training data [G], and learning algorithms are evaluated on training data
         [G].
  c33 : The provided corpus discusses several strategies for handling scarce training data or
         improving performance in data-limited settings: * **Unsupervised Pre-training:** Using
         unsupervised pre-training on a large corpus of data can facilitate good generalization in
         small-data settings [8]. For example, the SMILES Transformer learns molecular fingerprints

         generalizability to downstream tasks, even in zero-shot scenarios where no downstream data
         is used for refinement [10]. * **Domain-Specific Pre-training:** Models can be pre-trained
         on domain-specific text corpora (e.g., materials science) to improve performance [2]. *
